# Vulcanism and Earthquakes / India Seismic Zones — Mains-Focused Live Edition

This is the Mains edition of Geography Topic 03. It teaches the three geophysical systems that General Studies Paper I names by name — **volcanic activity, earthquakes and tsunami** — as physical processes first and as Indian risk second, and it then converts both into answer craft. Geography is a **named GS-I owner** of the clause "important Geophysical phenomena such as earthquakes, Tsunami, Volcanic activity, cyclone etc.", so this edition addresses the paper directly. ❗ The cyclone named in the same clause is **not** taught here; it has its own owner, and the clause is named rather than annexed.

**One topic, two halves, one file.** The world-process half and the India-risk half are separately examined, but they are not separate topics and they are not split into separate files. The viscosity lever built in Lesson 1 is the lever that explains why the Deccan is a plateau; the subduction architecture built in Lesson 6 is the architecture of the Andaman arc; the shallow-water wave theory derived in Lesson 12 is what makes the 2004 Indian coastline damage pattern intelligible rather than merely tragic. **Every one of Lessons 1 to 13 therefore closes on a named India hook — a specific place, event, institution or dataset, never the bare phrase "in India"** — and no hook is used twice. Lessons 14 and 15 carry the applied Indian argument, and Lesson 16 converts the whole file into examination technique.

**The honest shape of this topic, and it is unusual.** Four verified previous-year **Mains** demands are owned — 2018, 2020, 2021 and 2025 — which is the heaviest Mains load of any Geography topic in this lane so far, and they sit on four different directive words and four unrelated objects. Four objective demands are also owned. Yet **no 2026 General Studies Paper I demand reaches this topic**, and its only 2026 presence is a single provisional Prelims route. ❗ That is stated plainly rather than disguised: this edition is weighted towards **Mains answer craft, not recency**, and no recency claim is manufactured. Genuine currency comes from one real, dated governance episode at Lesson 15, which happens to be the best teaching asset in the topic.

**The zonation position, stated once at the front so that nothing later can be misread.** ✅ The operative Indian standard is **IS 1893 (Part 1): 2016**, with **four seismic zones, II, III, IV and V**. 📰 A revised standard, **IS 1893 (Part 1): 2025**, was introduced by Gazette notification on **6 November 2025** and introduced a new highest-risk **Zone VI**; it was **withdrawn by a fresh Gazette notification on 3 March 2026**, restoring the 2016 standard with immediate effect. ❗ **Zone VI is therefore taught in this file only as a dated, withdrawn proposal, never as the current map**, and the evidentiary limit of that account is disclosed at Lesson 15 and again in the source ledger.

**Answer-neutrality.** Objective demands appear with their year, paper, question number and conceptual demand only. **No answer letter, truth marking, elimination cue or corrective option mapping appears anywhere in this file** — including for the demand whose official Set-A key is physically held in the repository, and including for the 2026 demand whose key is provisional. What is taught is the *mechanism* each question tests, because that is what a Mains answer can use. No solved previous-year model answer appears; every model answer here is **original** and marked as such.

**What is deliberately left out.** Disaster-response doctrine and the institutional architecture of disaster administration; cyclones; glacial lake outburst floods; coastal regulation and island development; geopark and geoheritage designation; earthquake safety drills; compiled four-option objective corpora; and two book tables whose internal contradictions make them unsafe to quote. Each exclusion is recorded with its reason in the coverage matrix. ❗ Nothing that is a definition, a causal step, a derivation, an objection, a reply, a qualification or a practice item has been removed.

## Roadmap

Sixteen lessons. The count is not a quota; it is what four independent inputs produce. **One**, three directly owned syllabus clauses, each naming a *different physical system* — volcanic activity, earthquakes, tsunami — plus a fourth support clause that demands a risk-framing capability without the institutional content. **Two**, four owned Mains demands on four different objects with four different directive words, plus four objective demands on four further sub-objects: eight distinct answer targets. **Three**, a causal dependency chain that cannot be reordered without breaking, running from the heat budget to answer craft through two parallel strands that converge at the coast. **Four**, a mapwork obligation of ten distinct map and figure assets, which will not distribute across a smaller lesson set without crowding two unrelated visual grammars into one lesson.

## Lesson 1 — Why the Earth is restless: heat, melting and the birth of magma

Progress: 1 / 16  |  Stage: Foundation  |  Subtopic: The internal heat budget, the three routes to partial melting, and the silica lever that controls every landform later in the topic

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §1 and §8.1; G.C. Leong, Chapter 3, local-scan pp.23-24 (geothermal gradient, the Vulcan etymology, magmatic gases, basic against acid lava with temperatures and flow rates); Majid Husain, *Indian & World Geography*, printed p.2.15 (volcano defined as a landform at the end of a conduit; magma, lava and tephra separated).
CA search: "magma generation partial melting mantle India official statement 2026" — run for this lesson.
CA found: None in last 6 months. The file's single current linkage is carried at Lesson 15, where it belongs to a governance argument; a lesson on why rock melts gains nothing from a headline and inventing one would be a worse failure than recording a nil return.

```text
   temperature ------------------------------------------>

     |    GEOTHERM              SOLIDUS   .
  d  |   (actual T at             (T at  .

  h  |         \              to melt)             at depth is still SOLID

  |  |           \         .
  v  |            \      .    [A] DECOMPRESSION  -> move the rock UP at
     |             \   .          constant T; it crosses the solidus
     |              \ .           because the solidus falls with pressure
     |               X            = ridges, rifts, plume heads
     |              . \
     |            .    \      [B] FLUX / WATER   -> add water from a
     |          .       \         dehydrating slab; the solidus SHIFTS
     |        .          \        LEFT to lower T = subduction arcs
     |      .             \
     |    .                \  [C] HEAT TRANSFER  -> raise T at constant
     |  .                   \      depth by intruding hotter magma
     +------------------------\--  = crustal melting above a chamber

   RESULT: PARTIAL melt, a few per cent of the rock, LESS DENSE than the
   solid residue  ->  buoyant ascent  ->  magma chamber  ->  conduit  ->
   vent  ->  LAVA + GASES + TEPHRA at the surface
```

Two curves and three ways to make them cross. Everything in Lessons 2 to 7 is a consequence of which crossing happened, and of what the resulting melt was made of.

### 1. The question this lesson answers, and why it must come before any terminology

⚠️ Most treatments of vulcanism open with a list of volcano types. That is the wrong end of the causal chain, because a volcano type is an *outcome* and the examiner's better questions are about *mechanism*. The prior question is simpler and far more productive: **the Earth's interior is hot, but it is overwhelmingly solid — so why does any melt exist at all, and why does it exist in particular places?** An answer that can state the three routes to melting can derive the global distribution of volcanoes in Lesson 5 instead of memorising it.

⚠️ A second reason for this ordering is terminological hygiene. **Vulcanism is a process; a volcano is a landform; volcanic hazard is an outcome in a populated place.** Collapsing the three is the error that produces the one-sided disaster narrative which Lesson 7's demand explicitly penalises.

### 2. The heat budget, in the source's own units and with the correction attached

✅ G.C. Leong records the observation from which the whole subject begins: temperature rises as one descends into the Earth, at a geothermal gradient he gives as about **1 °F per 65 feet** of descent. ⚠️ In metric terms that is of the order of **25 °C per kilometre** in the uppermost crust — the conversion is given once, here, and thereafter the SI form is used. ❗ Quote Leong's figure **as Leong's**, flagged as a mid-century textbook statement in imperial units; do not present an unattributed converted number as current geophysics, and do not extrapolate the near-surface gradient to great depth, because it flattens dramatically below the lithosphere.

✅ Leong also supplies the etymology that fixes the vocabulary: **vulcanism takes its name from Vulcan, the Roman god of fire, by way of the island of Vulcano off Sicily**. ✅ And he records the gas inventory that accompanies magma — **carbon dioxide, sulphuretted hydrogen, nitrogen and chlorine** among them. ⚠️ Those gases matter twice over: they drive explosivity at Lesson 3 and they drive the climate-forcing pathway at Lesson 7.

⚠️ Where does the heat come from? Two sources, and an answer that names both is immediately better than one that names neither. **Primordial heat** is residual energy from accretion and from the differentiation that separated a metallic core — 🔁 that differentiation event is owned by the planetary topic and is assumed here, not re-derived. **Radiogenic heat** is produced continuously by the decay of long-lived unstable isotopes in the mantle and crust. ❗ Do not attach a percentage split between the two from memory; the partition is model-dependent and a wrong figure is identifiable with certainty.

### 3. Why hot rock is not molten rock — the point most answers skip

⚠️ Here is the step that converts a description into an explanation. **A rock's melting temperature is not a constant; it rises with pressure.** Deep in the mantle the rock is extremely hot, but it is also under extreme pressure, and the pressure raises the melting point faster than depth raises the temperature. The actual temperature profile with depth — the **geotherm** — therefore lies *below* the melting curve — the **solidus** — almost everywhere. That is the single sentence that explains why a hot planet is a solid planet.

⚠️ Melting therefore requires an event that makes the two curves cross. There are exactly three, and the diagram above is their derivation.

| Route | What changes | Where it operates | The volcanism it produces |
|---|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ **Decompression melting** | Pressure falls at roughly constant temperature, so the solidus falls below the geotherm | Mid-ocean ridges; continental rifts; the arrival of a buoyant plume head | Effusive, basaltic, high-volume; shields and lava plateaux |
| ⚠️ **Flux melting** | Water and other volatiles released from a descending slab shift the solidus to a lower temperature | Subduction zones, in the mantle wedge above the descending plate | Volatile-rich, more silicic, explosive; arc volcanoes |
| ⚠️ **Heat-transfer melting** | Temperature rises at constant pressure because hotter magma intrudes cooler crust | Above crustal magma chambers; at the base of thickened continental crust | Silicic melts; calderas and viscous domes |

❗ The examinable payoff is immediate. **A continent-continent collision belt has none of the three.** No slab descends to dehydrate, so there is no flux; the crust is thickening rather than decompressing, so there is no decompression melt at the relevant scale; and there is no plume beneath it. That is why the next sentence in this file is one of the most quotable in the topic.

✅ G.C. Leong states the observation flatly: **"The Himalayas have, surprisingly, no active volcano at all."** ⚠️ The book supplies the observation; this edition supplies the mechanism — **no subducting oceanic slab, therefore no flux melting, therefore a belt that is intensely seismic and volcanically silent.** That pairing is the entire answer to the standing directive "Why is the Himalaya seismic but not volcanic?", and it is built here, reinforced at Lesson 5 and Lesson 6, and deployed at Lesson 16.

### 4. From melt to landform: the vocabulary, separated once and kept separate

✅ Majid Husain's definition is exact and is worth reproducing as a structure rather than as a sentence: a **volcano is a landform at the end of a conduit or pipe which rises from below the crust to a vent at the surface**; magma rises and collects in a **magma chamber** deep below, and the eruption that follows is **effusive or explosive**.

| ✅ Term | What it denotes | The error it prevents |
|---|---|---|
| **Magma** | Molten rock **below** the surface, with its dissolved gases still in solution | Using "lava" for a subsurface body, which makes every intrusive form in Lesson 2 unintelligible |
| **Lava** | ✅ "The magma that actually issues from the volcano" — molten rock **at** the surface, substantially degassed | Treating eruption temperature and chamber composition as the same thing |
| **Tephra** | ✅ "Pulverised rock and clastic materials ejected violently during eruption" | Calling every fragment "ash"; ash is one size class within tephra |
| **Vent / conduit / pipe** | The opening and the pathway connecting chamber to surface | Confusing the *opening* with the *crater*, which is the depression around it |
| **Magma chamber** | The subsurface reservoir where melt accumulates and evolves | Imagining a continuous column of liquid from the mantle to the summit |

⚠️ One further distinction earns marks and costs nothing: **the melt that leaves a chamber is not the melt that entered it.** Crystals settle out, the residual liquid becomes progressively richer in silica, and the chamber therefore evolves. That is why a single volcano can erupt effusively at one stage and explosively at another, and why "a volcano has a fixed eruption type" is false.

### 5. The silica lever — the one variable that controls the rest of this topic

✅ Leong gives the contrast with numbers. **Basic lava** is rich in iron and magnesium and poor in silica; it emerges at about **1,000 °C (1,830 °F)**, is extremely mobile, and **flows at rates he gives as 10 to 30 miles per hour**, spreading far before it congeals. **Acid lava** is silica-rich and viscous; it cannot flow far, and it congeals quickly to build steep cones, spines and plugs — his type cases being **Mt Pelée** and the **Puy de Dôme**.

⚠️ The mechanism behind the contrast is worth one sentence because it converts two adjectives into a causal law. **Silicon-oxygen tetrahedra polymerise into chains and networks in the melt; the more silica, the more extensive the polymerisation, and the more viscous the liquid.** High viscosity does two things at once: it slows flow, and it traps exsolving gas until the pressure is released catastrophically. ❗ **Viscosity therefore controls both the landform and the violence**, which is why the same variable reappears in Lesson 3 as the eruption-style ladder and in Lesson 4 as the cone-against-plateau contrast.

```text
   LOW SILICA  ------------------ the single lever ------------------>  HIGH SILICA
   basic / basaltic                                                  acid / rhyolitic
   ~1,000 C (1,830 F) at the vent                                    cooler at the vent
   LOW viscosity, flows 10-30 mph                                    HIGH viscosity
   gas escapes freely                                                gas is TRAPPED
        |                                                                  |
        v                                                                  v
   QUIET effusive eruption                                           VIOLENT explosive
   broad SHIELDS, LAVA PLATEAUX                                      STEEP cones, SPINES,
   fissure sheets                                                    PLUGS, CALDERAS
   example: Deccan, Iceland, Hawaii                                  example: Pelee, Puy de Dome
```

### 6. Vulcanism as a process against volcanic hazard as an outcome

⚠️ One more separation, because the 2021 demand turns on it. **Vulcanism is a planetary heat-transfer process.** It has operated for the whole of Earth history, it builds crust, it vents volatiles, and over geological time it is constructive. **Volcanic hazard exists only where the process intersects a population.** The same eruption is a geological event on an uninhabited island and a catastrophe in a dense valley, and nothing about the magma changed between the two cases.

❗ This is not a pious observation; it is the structural reason why Lesson 7 can argue a *balance* rather than list disasters, and why the examiner's phrase "environmental impact" must be read as including soil, geothermal energy, minerals and landscape, not only ash and death.

### 7. 🇮🇳 India hook — the Deccan is a plateau, not a cone, and the reason is in this lesson

🇮🇳 India's largest volcanic province carries no volcanic peak. ⚠️ The **Deccan** is a **plateau** of stacked, flat-lying flows with step-like scarps, and the Core owner states the trap explicitly: flood basalts are **not** ordinary cone eruptions scaled up, but **fissure** eruptions of very fluid lava that build plateaux rather than peaks. ✅ Leong's own list of lava plateaux names the **north-west Deccan** alongside the Snake Basin and Iceland, which is the independent corroboration that the form is a general consequence of lava type and not an Indian peculiarity.

⚠️ Run the lever backwards from the landform and the inference is exact: a plateau of enormous lateral extent and negligible relief requires lava of very low viscosity, therefore low silica, therefore basic composition, therefore a source that melted by **decompression** rather than by flux. Lesson 5 supplies the candidate source; this lesson supplies the reasoning that makes the candidate necessary.

❗ **Limitation, stated with the hook.** The Deccan's **emplacement as stratigraphy** — its date bracket, thickness structure, original and residual extent, and its place in India's rock systems — 🔁 belongs to the crust-and-rocks topic and is not re-taught here. Topic 03 takes from the Deccan exactly one thing: it is the standing Indian demonstration that **lava chemistry, not eruption violence, decides the landform**.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| | What it explains | What it does not explain |
|---|---|---|
| The geotherm-solidus relation | Why a hot planet is a solid planet, and why melting needs an event | Where the heat itself comes from — that is the partition between primordial and radiogenic heat, left unquantified here |
| The three melting routes | Why volcanoes occur where they occur, including away from boundaries | Why a particular volcano erupts in a particular year; eruption *timing* is a monitoring question, not a mechanism question |
| The silica-viscosity lever | Landform family and eruption violence together, from one variable | The absolute volume of an eruption, which depends on melt supply and chamber size rather than on composition |

**Strongest objection.** This is petrology, not geography. A General Studies candidate has no use for polymerised tetrahedra.

**Reply.** The lever is not taught for its chemistry; it is taught because it is the **cheapest possible way to make four later lessons derivable instead of memorisable**. One variable predicts the eruption-style ladder of Lesson 3, the cone-against-plateau split of Lesson 4, the contrast between effusive ridge volcanism and explosive arc volcanism in Lesson 5, and the asymmetry of hazard in Lesson 7. ❗ An answer that writes "basic lava is fluid because it is low in silica, so it builds plateaux and erupts quietly" has spent twenty words and bought four paragraphs of consequence.

⚠️ **Residual.** The three melting routes are the standard account and are not seriously contested, but the **relative contribution** of each to global melt production is model-dependent, and the depth and nature of mantle thermal anomalies remain actively debated — which is exactly the unresolved residual that Lesson 5 develops into an argument tree.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** "Salient features of world's physical geography" and "important Geophysical phenomena such as earthquakes, Tsunami, Volcanic activity" (Mains GS-I, direct ownership); the Prelims physical-geography clause.
- **Verified linkage:** no demand is owned by this lesson alone. It is the premise lesson for the **2018** mantle-plume demand, the **2020** Circum-Pacific demand and the **2021** eruption-impact demand, and it supplies the concept route for the **2024** objective demand on eruption products.
- **Probable framing:** this material appears in a Mains answer as a **two-sentence foundation** — melting requires decompression, flux or heat transfer; silica content then fixes viscosity, and viscosity fixes both landform and violence — and almost never as the demand itself.
- **Traps:** describing the mantle as molten; treating the geothermal gradient as linear to the core; presenting a volcano type as a cause rather than an effect; quoting Leong's imperial figures as current metric values; calling every fragment "ash"; assuming a volcano has one permanent eruption style.
- **Answer use:** open a volcanism answer with the lever, not with a definition. *"Because silica content fixes melt viscosity, and viscosity fixes both the distance lava travels and the ease with which gas escapes, a single compositional variable predicts the landform and the violence together."* That sentence survives into any of the four owned Mains answers.

Revision notes:

- **Geothermal gradient** — ✅ Leong: about **1 °F per 65 feet**; ⚠️ of the order of **25 °C per km** in the uppermost crust. ❗ Attribute the imperial figure; do not extrapolate to depth.
- Heat sources: **primordial** (accretion and differentiation) + **radiogenic** (long-lived isotope decay). ❗ No percentage split from memory.
- ⚠️ **Melting point rises with pressure**; the geotherm normally lies below the solidus — **a hot planet is a solid planet**.
- **Three melting routes:** **decompression** (ridges, rifts, plume heads) · **flux/water** (subduction arcs) · **heat transfer** (crustal melting above chambers).
- ❗ A **collision belt has none of the three** → ✅ Leong: **"The Himalayas have, surprisingly, no active volcano at all."** Seismic, not volcanic.
- ✅ **Magma** = below surface; **lava** = "the magma that actually issues from the volcano"; **tephra** = "pulverised rock and clastic materials ejected violently"; **vent/conduit/chamber** distinguished.
- ⚠️ A chamber **evolves** — crystal settling enriches the residual liquid in silica, so eruption style can change within one volcano's history.
- ✅ **Basic lava** ~**1,000 °C (1,830 °F)**, iron- and magnesium-rich, silica-poor, flows **10-30 mph** → shields and plateaux. **Acid lava** viscous → steep cones, **spines**, **plugs** (Pelée, Puy de Dôme).
- ⚠️ Mechanism: silica polymerises the melt → **high viscosity traps gas** → explosivity. **One lever, two outputs: landform and violence.**
- ✅ Etymology: **Vulcan**, Roman god of fire, via the island of **Vulcano** off Sicily. ✅ Magmatic gases: **CO2, sulphuretted hydrogen, nitrogen, chlorine**.
- ⚠️ **Vulcanism is a process; a volcano is a landform; volcanic hazard is an outcome in a populated place.** Do not collapse the three.
- 🇮🇳 **The Deccan is a plateau, not a cone** — ⚠️ fissure eruption of very fluid lava; ✅ Leong lists the **north-west Deccan** among lava plateaux with the Snake Basin and Iceland. 🔁 Its stratigraphy belongs to the crust-and-rocks topic.
- ❗ Run the lever backwards: plateau → low viscosity → low silica → **decompression** source. The landform is evidence about the melt.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "Volcanoes occur because the interior of the Earth is molten, and the molten rock forces its way out through weak points in the crust." Identify the errors and give a corrected two-sentence statement that would earn marks in a ten-mark answer.

**Model answer:** Two errors, one of fact and one of mechanism. The factual error is "molten": the mantle is extremely hot but overwhelmingly **solid**, because pressure raises the melting temperature faster than depth raises the actual temperature, so the geotherm lies below the solidus almost everywhere. The mechanism error is "weak points": weakness of the crust may determine the **pathway** a melt takes, but it cannot determine the **existence** of melt, and distribution is governed by where melting occurs, not by where the crust happens to be fractured. A corrected statement: *"The mantle is hot but solid, because pressure raises its melting point; melt is produced only where decompression, the addition of water from a descending slab, or the intrusion of hotter material causes the rock to cross its solidus."* And: *"The resulting partial melt is less dense than the residue, so it rises buoyantly to a magma chamber and erupts through a conduit, with its silica content deciding whether the eruption is quiet and far-flowing or viscous and explosive."*

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat "hot" and "molten" as interchangeable merely because the interior is hot enough to flow slowly. Solids near their melting point deform over geological time without being liquid, and the distinction is what makes decompression melting at ridges and plume heads an explanation rather than a restatement.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "Partial melting, not internal heat, is what makes a volcano possible." Examine. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** The statement is sound. Internal heat is necessary but not sufficient, because the melting temperature of mantle rock rises with pressure faster than the actual temperature rises with depth, so the hot interior remains overwhelmingly solid. Melt appears only where the solidus is crossed, and there are three such routes: decompression, where material rises at near-constant temperature beneath ridges, rifts and plume heads; flux melting, where water driven from a descending slab lowers the solidus in the overlying wedge; and heat transfer, where intruding magma raises the temperature of cooler crust. The product is a partial melt, a small fraction of the rock, less dense than the residue and therefore buoyant. Its silica content then decides viscosity, and viscosity decides both landform and violence. The qualification is that a collision belt satisfies none of the three routes, which is why the Himalaya is intensely seismic and volcanically silent.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 2 for the pressure-dependence of the melting point stated as the reason a hot interior stays solid; 3 for all three melting routes named with their settings; 2 for partial melt's buoyancy as the ascent mechanism; 2 for silica content linked forward to viscosity, landform and violence; 1 for the collision-belt case used as the qualification. Deduct 2 for describing the mantle as molten and 1 for naming only subduction as a melting route.

## Lesson 2 — Intrusive vulcanism: the forms magma takes when it never reaches the surface

Progress: 2 / 16  |  Stage: Foundation  |  Subtopic: Concordant and discordant plutonic bodies, their type-localities, and how an intrusion becomes a landform long after the magma has cooled

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §1 (plutonic against volcanic; the rock-cycle premise); G.C. Leong, Chapter 3, local-scan pp.24-25 (sill, dyke, laccolith, lopolith, phacolith and batholith with named type-localities, and the end-of-chapter comparative pairs at local-scan p.29).
CA search: "dyke swarm intrusion India geological survey report 2026" — run for this lesson.
CA found: None in last 6 months. No current item is attached here; the lesson's evidence is structural and the file's one current linkage is reserved for Lesson 15.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

```text
   A SINGLE CRUSTAL BLOCK, CARRYING EVERY INTRUSIVE FORM
   ground surface ~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~
        |                 ^^^^^^ exhumed dyke stands as a WALL
        |                 ||
   ===============================================================  bedding
   ~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~ SILL ~~~~~~~~~~~~  (CONCORDANT: parallel to bedding)
   ===============================================================
            ||  <- DYKE (DISCORDANT: cuts across bedding)
   =========||====================================================
            ||        ___---^^^---___
            ||   ____/  LACCOLITH    \____   (CONCORDANT: floor flat,
   =========||==/   roof DOMED upward     \==  roof arched; viscous magma)
            ||
   ======\\=||=//=================================================
          \\ || //   LOPOLITH   (CONCORDANT: saucer / basin shaped,
           \\||//                SAGGING downward; very large, basic)
            \||/
   ----------\/---------------------------------------------------
        fold crest  /\        fold trough   \/
                   /PH\  PHACOLITH fills     \PH/   (CONCORDANT: lens in
                  /    \  the crest or trough  \/     an ANTICLINE or SYNCLINE)
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
        /                                              \
       /          B A T H O L I T H                     \   (DISCORDANT:
      /     enormous, deep-seated, NO OBSERVABLE BOTTOM   \   the roots of
     /______________________________________________________\  a mountain belt)
```

Six forms, one block. Read the diagram by a single question each time: **does the body follow the bedding or cut across it?** That one test sorts the whole family.

### 1. Why an intrusive lesson exists in a topic about eruptions

⚠️ A candidate's instinct is that vulcanism means eruption. It does not. **Most magma never reaches the surface**; it solidifies at depth, and the bodies it forms are both the plumbing of the volcanoes that do erupt and, after millions of years of denudation, landforms in their own right. Three consequences follow, and they are why this lesson cannot be folded into Lesson 3.

- ⚠️ **The intrusive body is the record of the eruption's supply system.** A dyke swarm is the fossilised set of feeder fractures of a flood-basalt province; the dykes are the pipes, the flows are the product.
- ⚠️ **Intrusions become relief by inversion.** A resistant igneous body emplaced into softer country rock is initially invisible at the surface. Denudation removes the softer rock preferentially, and the intrusion is left standing — a wall, a ridge, a dome or an upland. ❗ The landform is therefore produced by **erosion acting on a contrast**, not by the intrusion "pushing up" the surface, and candidates routinely write the second.
- ⚠️ **The forms carry high-value terminology.** Lopolith and phacolith are the two intrusive terms most often confused, and a question that names them rewards the candidate who can attach a shape and a type-locality to each.

### 2. The sorting test, applied: concordant against discordant

| Form | Concordant or discordant | Shape and emplacement | ✅ Named type-localities (Leong) |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Sill** | **Concordant** | Sheet injected **along** a bedding plane; may be many kilometres in extent and a few metres thick | — |
| **Dyke** | **Discordant** | Sheet injected **across** bedding, commonly near-vertical; feeds flows and sills | **Cleveland Dyke, Yorkshire**; a **quartzite dyke north of Kuala Lumpur** |
| **Laccolith** | **Concordant** | Flat-floored, **dome-roofed** body; viscous magma ponds and arches the strata above it | **Henry Mountains, Utah** |
| **Lopolith** | **Concordant** | **Saucer- or basin-shaped**, sagging **downward**; characteristically very large and basic | **Bushveld, Transvaal** |
| **Phacolith** | **Concordant** | **Lens-shaped** body occupying the **crest of an anticline or the trough of a syncline** | **Corndon Hill, Shropshire** |
| **Batholith** | **Discordant** | Enormous deep-seated mass with **no observable bottom**; the cooled roots of a mountain belt | **Wicklow Mountains**; **Brittany**; the **Main Range of West Malaysia** |

⚠️ **The pair candidates confuse, resolved in one line each.** A **laccolith arches upward** because viscous magma cannot spread, so it lifts its roof; a **lopolith sags downward** because a very large body of dense basic magma loads and depresses its floor as it crystallises. ❗ Up-dome against down-sag: that is the whole distinction, and attaching **Henry Mountains** to the first and **Bushveld** to the second makes it permanent.

⚠️ **Why a phacolith is a different kind of fact.** The sill, the dyke, the laccolith and the lopolith are shapes a magma body takes in response to its own properties. A **phacolith is a shape imposed by the host structure**: when beds are folded, low-pressure openings develop in the crest of an anticline and the trough of a syncline, and magma migrates into them. ❗ It is therefore evidence that **intrusion and folding were broadly contemporaneous**, which is a chronological statement, not merely a morphological one.

### 3. Grain size, depth and the texture rule — the reason "plutonic" is not a synonym for "big"

⚠️ The Core owner's classification runs on a simple physical rule. **Crystal size varies inversely with cooling rate, and cooling rate is set by the depth of emplacement.** A batholith, insulated by kilometres of country rock, cools over geological time and grows large, visible crystals; a thin dyke in cold rock chills quickly and is fine-grained; the same magma reaching the surface as a lava flow is finer still and may be glassy.

❗ Two consequences that an examiner can test. First, **a single body can be coarse at its centre and fine at its chilled margin**, so texture is a statement about a *sample*, not about a *province*. Second, **a coarse-grained rock now exposed at the surface carries an additional inference**: it must have been emplaced at depth and then uncovered, which requires a long denudational history. ⚠️ That second inference is the one that turns a rock name into a landscape argument.

### 4. From intrusion to landscape: inversion of relief, worked through

```text
   STAGE 1  emplacement        STAGE 2  denudation       STAGE 3  inversion
   ------------------------    --------------------      --------------------
   soft country rock           softer material is        resistant intrusion
   ####################        stripped preferentially   stands PROUD
   ####[ DYKE ]########           ####  ||  ####              ||
   ####[      ]########             ##  ||  ##                ||  <- WALL
   ####################               \ || /              ____||____
   dyke is BURIED and                  \||/              /          \
   topographically invisible            ||              /  country rock
                                                       /   lowered around it
   THE RULE: relief is produced by DIFFERENTIAL EROSION of a resistance
   contrast. The intrusion did not rise; everything else came down.
```

⚠️ The same logic runs in the opposite direction where the intrusion is **less** resistant than its host, in which case the dyke weathers out as a trench rather than standing as a wall. ❗ That is why the correct formulation is always **resistance contrast**, never "hard rock makes high ground" — and it is the same reasoning the crust-and-rocks topic applies to permeability.

### 5. The intrusive forms as evidence rather than as vocabulary

⚠️ A list of six forms is worth very little in a Mains answer. The same six forms, used as **evidence**, are worth a great deal, and three uses are available.

- ⚠️ **Evidence of magma viscosity.** A laccolith implies magma too viscous to spread laterally; an extensive sill implies magma fluid enough to travel far along a bedding plane. The intrusive geometry therefore records the same silica lever that Lesson 1 installed.
- ⚠️ **Evidence of structural timing.** A phacolith in a fold crest implies intrusion during or after folding; a dyke cutting a fold implies intrusion after it; a folded sill implies intrusion before it. ❗ This is the cross-cutting principle, and 🔁 its full development as a dating toolkit belongs to the crust-and-rocks topic, which is named here once and not duplicated.
- ⚠️ **Evidence of a former volcanic system.** A radial dyke set around a central plug is the eroded skeleton of a volcano whose cone has gone entirely. ✅ Majid's own figure of volcanic features draws precisely this — an **eroded volcanic plug** with a **radial dyke** — which is the bridge between this lesson and Lesson 4's discussion of plugs and spines.

### 6. 🇮🇳 India hook — the Deccan dyke swarms, and the Malani igneous suite as a batholithic analogue

🇮🇳 India supplies both halves of this lesson at the largest available scale. ⚠️ The **Deccan dyke swarms** — dense sets of near-vertical basaltic sheets cutting the country rock in the western Deccan and along the Narmada-Tapi corridor — are the **feeder system** of the flood-basalt province that Lesson 1 used as its plateau case. The relationship is exact and is worth stating as such in an answer: **the dykes are the pipes, the traps are the product**, and the swarm's geometry records the fracture pattern through which the lava reached the surface.

🇮🇳 At the opposite end of the family, the **Malani igneous suite** of the north-western Peninsula is India's standing **batholithic analogue**: a very large, deep-seated acid igneous mass, now exposed by prolonged denudation, whose granitic character is the Indian counterpart of Leong's Wicklow and Brittany cases.

❗ **Limitations, both stated with the hook and both non-negotiable.** First, **no volume, extent, thickness or emplacement date is asserted here for either body**; those are stratigraphic quantities, 🔁 owned by the crust-and-rocks topic, and an invented figure is the kind of error an examiner identifies with certainty. Second, the hook is used for exactly one purpose — to show that **intrusive and extrusive forms are two expressions of one magmatic system**, which is the point a ten-mark answer on eruption products can bank.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| | Concordant family | Discordant family |
|---|---|---|
| Relation to host | Follows bedding or foliation | Cuts across it |
| Members | Sill, laccolith, lopolith, phacolith | Dyke, batholith |
| What the geometry records | The **host structure's** control on emplacement | The **magma's** ability to fracture and traverse the host |
| Typical landscape expression on exhumation | Benches, domed uplands, basin-centred masses | Walls and ridges; eroded uplands over a batholith |
| Dating value | Phacolith constrains intrusion relative to folding | Dyke cutting a structure gives a clean "after" relation |

**Strongest objection.** These are names on a diagram. No Mains question has ever asked for a lopolith.

**Reply.** True, and irrelevant. The forms are not taught to be recalled as a list; they are taught because three separate demands need them as **supporting evidence**. An eruption-products question needs the distinction between what solidifies below and what is ejected above. A plume question needs the plume head's enormous shallow intrusive and extrusive output to be describable. And any answer that explains **why the Deccan looks the way it does** needs the feeder-dyke relation. ❗ The correct discipline is to **hold the vocabulary, deploy one or two terms with a type-locality, and move on** — not to recite six forms in a 150-word answer.

⚠️ **Residual.** Intrusive geometry constrains magma viscosity and structural timing, but **not** absolute age: a dyke that cuts a fold is younger than the fold by an unknown interval, which may be a thousand years or a hundred million. ❗ This file asserts no radiometric date for any Indian intrusive body.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** "Salient features of world's physical geography" and the volcanic-activity element of the geophysical-phenomena clause (Mains GS-I, direct ownership).
- **Verified linkage:** supports the **2024** objective demand on the **products of volcanic eruptions** — the intrusive family is the half of the answer that candidates omit — and underpins the **2018** plume demand, where a plume head's output is both intrusive and extrusive. *No answer key is recorded or inferred for the objective demand.*
- **Probable framing:** intrusive forms appear in a Mains script as **one clause inside a larger answer**, most often as the feeder relation behind a named province. They are almost never the demand.
- **Traps:** saying the intrusion "pushed the land up" instead of invoking differential erosion; reversing the laccolith and lopolith geometries; treating "plutonic" as a synonym for large; assigning a coarse texture to a chilled dyke margin; quoting an Indian intrusive body's age or volume from memory.
- **Answer use:** one sentence buys the relation. *"The Deccan's feeder dykes and its flood basalts are a single magmatic system expressed above and below the surface, which is why the province has no cone: the magma was delivered through fissures, not a central vent."*

Revision notes:

- ⚠️ **Most magma never erupts.** Intrusive bodies are the plumbing of eruptions and become landforms by **inversion of relief**.
- **The sorting test:** does the body **follow** bedding (**concordant**) or **cut across** it (**discordant**)?
- **Concordant:** **sill** (sheet along bedding) · **laccolith** (flat floor, **domed roof**, viscous magma) · **lopolith** (**saucer, sags downward**, large and basic) · **phacolith** (**lens in a fold crest or trough**).
- **Discordant:** **dyke** (cuts bedding, commonly vertical, feeds flows) · **batholith** (enormous, deep-seated, **no observable bottom**).
- ✅ Type-localities: **Cleveland Dyke, Yorkshire**; **quartzite dyke north of Kuala Lumpur**; **Henry Mountains, Utah** (laccolith); **Bushveld, Transvaal** (lopolith); **Corndon Hill, Shropshire** (phacolith); **Wicklow Mountains**, **Brittany**, **Main Range of West Malaysia** (batholiths).
- ❗ **Laccolith arches UP; lopolith sags DOWN.** Henry against Bushveld.
- ⚠️ A **phacolith** is shaped by the **host structure**, so it is evidence that intrusion and **folding** were broadly contemporaneous.
- ⚠️ **Crystal size varies inversely with cooling rate**; cooling rate is set by **depth**. A body can be coarse at its centre and fine at its **chilled margin**.
- ⚠️ A coarse rock now at the surface implies **emplacement at depth plus prolonged denudation** — a landscape inference, not just a rock name.
- ⚠️ Relief comes from **differential erosion of a resistance contrast**; the intrusion did not rise, the surroundings came down. A less resistant dyke weathers out as a **trench**.
- ⚠️ Three evidential uses: **viscosity** (laccolith against extensive sill) · **structural timing** (phacolith, cross-cutting) · **a vanished volcano** (✅ Majid's eroded **plug** with **radial dykes**).
- 🇮🇳 **Deccan dyke swarms = the feeder pipes; the traps = the product.** 🇮🇳 **Malani igneous suite** = India's batholithic analogue. ❗ **No volume, extent or date asserted** — 🔁 stratigraphy belongs to the crust-and-rocks topic.
- ❗ In a 150-word answer, deploy **one or two forms with a type-locality**, never a recited list of six.

### Concept check

**Question:** A dyke stands as a wall several metres above the surrounding country, and in the same district a sill forms a flat bench. A candidate concludes that "the dyke was intruded with greater force than the sill." Explain why the conclusion does not follow, and state what the two landforms actually record.

**Model answer:** The conclusion confuses emplacement energy with present relief. Neither body rose to its present topographic position; both were emplaced within the rock column and were originally invisible at the surface. The relief is produced by **differential erosion of a resistance contrast**: where the igneous body resists weathering and erosion better than its host, the host is lowered around it and the intrusion is left standing. What the two landforms actually record is **geometry relative to bedding**. The dyke is **discordant** — it cut across the strata, which tells us the magma fractured its host and travelled on a transverse pathway, commonly feeding flows or sills above. The sill is **concordant** — it exploited a bedding plane, which tells us the magma was fluid enough to travel far laterally along an existing plane of weakness. A wall and a bench are therefore statements about **orientation and host control**, not about force; and had the dyke been less resistant than its host, the identical intrusion would today be a trench.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not read any exhumed intrusion as having "pushed up" the surface. The only intrusive form that genuinely deforms its roof during emplacement is the laccolith, which arches the overlying strata because viscous magma cannot spread — and even there, the present-day hill is the product of later denudation, not of the arching alone.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** Distinguish concordant from discordant intrusive bodies, and explain what each type reveals about the magma and the rocks it entered. *(Distinguish and explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** Concordant bodies are emplaced parallel to the existing structure of the host; discordant bodies cut across it. The concordant family comprises the sill, injected along a bedding plane; the laccolith, whose flat floor and domed roof record magma too viscous to spread; the lopolith, a large basin-shaped basic mass that sagged as it crystallised, typified by the Bushveld; and the phacolith, a lens occupying the crest of an anticline or the trough of a syncline, as at Corndon Hill. The discordant family comprises the dyke, which fractures its host and commonly feeds flows above, as the Cleveland Dyke does, and the batholith, a deep-seated mass with no observable bottom, forming the roots of mountain belts such as the Wicklow Mountains. Concordant geometry therefore reveals host control and magma mobility; discordant geometry reveals the magma's capacity to fracture its host. The limitation is that geometry constrains relative, never absolute, age.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 2 for the concordant/discordant criterion stated as a test rather than as a label; 3 for at least four forms correctly assigned with their geometry; 2 for two or more named type-localities attached correctly; 2 for the evidential reading — viscosity from the laccolith, host control from the phacolith, fracturing from the dyke; 1 for the relative-age limitation. Deduct 2 for reversing laccolith and lopolith and 1 for attributing relief to the intrusion rising.

## Lesson 3 — Extrusive vulcanism: lava, eruption style and what actually comes out

Progress: 3 / 16  |  Stage: Core  |  Subtopic: The 1908 fourfold eruption classification as a graded ladder, and the full inventory of eruption products

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §1 and §8.3 (eruption style varies with lava; the ash, gas, lahar and pyroclastic-density-current pathways); Majid Husain, *Indian & World Geography*, printed pp.2.15-2.17 (Lacroix's 1908 fourfold classification with Icelandic and Vesuvian added in the figure; tephra; cinder-cone size criterion); G.C. Leong, Chapter 3, local-scan pp.24-27 (basic against acid lava with temperatures and flow rates; the pyroclast family; lava tongues, lava-dammed lakes, lava bridges and tunnels; volcanic dust circling the globe); Majid Husain, *Geography of India*, printed p.2.42 (Barren Island).
