🇮🇳 India's wave-reading capability has a named oldest node. 📰 ✅ The **National Center for Seismology** records that the **Central Seismological Observatory (CSO), Shillong, was established in 1902 as the second observatory in India**, and that it *"maintains a 20 station network of northeast India"* as part of the national network. 📰 ✅ The national network itself — the **National Seismological Network (NSN)** — is recorded as comprising **more than 170 stations**, with *"earthquake monitoring on a 24x7 basis"* described as *"the prime activity of the center"*, supplying **real-time data for real-time monitoring across the country**, and supplemented by **temporary networks deployed for swarm or aftershock monitoring**.

⚠️ **Why Shillong is the right hook and not an arbitrary one.** The second-oldest observatory in the country was sited in the **north-east**, which is the most seismically active region in India (Lesson 14), and the twenty-station sub-network it maintains is a density response to that gradient. ❗ **Network geometry follows seismic geography** — a dense sub-network where events are frequent, a sparser national grid elsewhere, and mobile deployments where a swarm or an aftershock sequence demands temporary resolution. ⚠️ That is the §3 derivation turned into institutional design: because location requires three or more stations, **capability is a function of station spacing**, and spacing is decided by where the events are.

❗ **Boundaries and limits.** ⚠️ The station count is a **dated institutional statement from the Centre's own service description**, not a constant; networks grow. ❗ **No earthquake catalogue, event magnitude, detection threshold or location accuracy figure is asserted here.** 🔁 Instrumentation design, sensor technology and the engineering of warning systems belong to the science-and-technology owner; 🔁 the use of seismological advisories in relief administration belongs to the disaster-management owner. Topic 03 takes the network only as the **physical capability to locate and characterise an event**.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| | Body waves (P, S) | Surface waves (Love, Rayleigh) |
|---|---|---|
| Path | Through the Earth's interior | Along the near-surface |
| Speed | Fastest (P), intermediate (S) | Slowest |
| Amplitude at distance | Decays faster — energy spreads through a volume | Decays more slowly — energy spreads over a surface |
| Period | Shorter | **Longer**, and dispersive |
| Diagnostic use | Location, depth, internal structure | Magnitude at longer periods; damage assessment |
| Damage contribution | Jolt (P), shearing (S) | **Most structural damage** |

**Strongest objection.** Wave physics is a Prelims matter. A Mains answer on earthquake hazard gains nothing from particle motion.

**Reply.** It gains three things, each of which is a mark. ⚠️ **One**, the damage argument is a wave argument: it is the **long-period surface waves** that couple to building response, so an answer that explains why the last-arriving waves do the most damage has explained the damage rather than reported it. **Two**, the **early-warning capability** that Lesson 12 relies on is derived entirely from the speed difference between an electromagnetic signal and a seismic wave; without the wave hierarchy, warning is unintelligible. **Three**, the **three-station rule** is why seismological capacity is institutional rather than instrumental — which is the argument that makes a network docket relevant to a geography answer at all.

⚠️ **Residual.** Wave speeds vary with the material traversed, so the S-P-to-distance conversion uses an assumed velocity model; where the real structure departs from the model, locations are biased. ❗ This file asserts **no wave speed and no location-accuracy figure**.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** "important Geophysical phenomena such as earthquakes" (Mains GS-I, direct ownership); the Prelims physical-geography clause.
- **Verified linkage:** carries the **concept route** for the **2023** objective demand on **P-wave and S-wave properties**, and supplies the physical premise for the early-warning capability used at Lesson 12. ❗ *Answer-neutral: no key is recorded or inferred; no official 2023 key is held locally.*
- **Probable framing:** in a Mains answer, two sentences — the wave hierarchy and the fact that surface waves do the damage — plus, where relevant, one sentence on the network requirement for location.
- **Traps:** saying S waves cannot travel through liquids **or** gases without noting that the operative property is the absence of shear strength; assuming the first arrival is the most damaging; thinking one station can locate an earthquake; confusing early warning with prediction; quoting a wave speed or a station count as a constant; importing the shadow-zone argument about the core into a hazard answer.
- **Answer use:** *"The waves that arrive last do the most damage: long-period Love and Rayleigh surface waves decay slowly with distance and couple efficiently to the natural period of structures, which is why the duration and the ground conditions matter as much as the first jolt."*

Revision notes:

- 🔁 **Boundary:** waves **as evidence of internal structure** belong to the crust-and-rocks topic; waves **as shaking agents and as a location tool** belong here.
- ✅ **P (primary):** compressional, **fastest, first**, travels through **solids and liquids**; particle motion **along** the ray path.
- ✅ **S (secondary):** shear, slower, **solids only**; particle motion **perpendicular** to the ray path.
- ⚠️ **Love:** surface, **horizontal transverse**. ⚠️ **Rayleigh:** surface, **rolling, elliptical, retrograde**. ✅ Surface waves are slowest and cause **most of the structural damage**.
- ⚠️ **Why the slowest do the most damage:** larger amplitude, **slower decay** (energy over a surface, not a volume), and **long period** coupling to building response.
- ⚠️ **S cannot cross a liquid** because a liquid has no shear strength — the same property that makes a **liquefied layer** transmit no shear (Lesson 13).
- ⚠️ **Shadow zones:** P refracted and S stopped at the core boundary; the **S shadow is larger**. 🔁 Its structural use is the other topic's. ❗ Here it only explains a missing S phase at a remote station.
- **S-P derivation:** interval = d(1/vS − 1/vP) → **interval is proportional to distance**. ⚠️ One station = **distance, not direction**. ✅ **Location needs three or more stations.**
- ⚠️ Strictly three **spheres**, so the solution fixes **focus and depth together**; real solutions use many stations and carry **uncertainty**. ❗ A first-reported epicentre is **not final**.
- ❗ **A seismogram cannot predict.** ⚠️ **Early warning** is possible because electromagnetic transmission outruns seismic propagation — categorically different from prediction.
- ⚠️ **Detection counts depend on network sensitivity and year** — the technical basis of the counting caution at Lessons 6 and 8.
- **Objective concept set, held as contrasts:** compressional against shear · faster against slower · liquids-permitted against solids-only · along-path against perpendicular · body against surface. ❗ *Answer-neutral.*
- 🇮🇳 📰 ✅ **Central Seismological Observatory, Shillong — established 1902, the second observatory in India**, maintaining a **20-station north-east network**. 📰 ✅ **National Seismological Network: more than 170 stations, 24×7 monitoring, real-time data**, plus **temporary networks for swarms and aftershocks**.
- ⚠️ **Network geometry follows seismic geography**: dense where events are frequent, mobile where a sequence demands resolution. ❗ Station counts are **dated institutional statements**, not constants.

### Concept check

**Question:** A seismic station records a clear P arrival, a clear S arrival ninety seconds later, and strong long-period motion thereafter. A second station two thousand kilometres away records a P arrival and no S arrival at all. Explain what the first station can and cannot determine on its own, and give two distinct explanations for the second station's missing phase.

**Model answer:** From the S-P interval alone the first station can determine its **distance** from the focus, because the interval equals the path length multiplied by the difference of the reciprocals of the S and P velocities and is therefore proportional to distance for a given velocity model. It cannot determine **direction**, so its information defines a circle — strictly a sphere, since the focus has depth — rather than a point; location requires arrival times at **three or more stations**, whose intersecting surfaces fix epicentre and depth together. The strong long-period motion after the S arrival is the **Love and Rayleigh surface-wave train**, which carries most of the damaging energy. For the second station, two explanations are available and they are different in kind. The first is **geometric**: if the path to that station passes through the core's shadow geometry, the S phase is not transmitted at all, because S waves cannot propagate through a liquid, and the S shadow is the larger of the two. The second is **instrumental or signal-related**: a low signal-to-noise ratio, an inappropriate instrument response or a small event at long range can bury the S arrival in background noise even where the geometry permits it. The first is a property of the Earth; the second is a property of the observation.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat a missing phase as a measurement error by default. In seismology an absence can be the strongest kind of evidence — the S shadow is the classic case — so the first question about a missing arrival is always whether the geometry permits it, not whether the instrument failed.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** Explain how the properties of seismic waves make it possible to locate an earthquake, and why that capability is institutional rather than instrumental. *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** Location rests on a speed difference. P waves are compressional, travel through solids and liquids and arrive first; S waves are shear waves, require rigidity, travel more slowly and arrive later. The gap between the two arrivals equals the path length multiplied by the difference of the reciprocals of the two velocities, so the S-minus-P interval is proportional to distance from the focus. A single station can convert its record into a distance, but not a direction: its information defines a circle on the surface, or a sphere once focal depth is admitted. Only where three or more stations each supply such a surface do the surfaces intersect at a unique point, fixing epicentre and depth together. Capability is therefore a property of the **network**, not of any instrument, which is why seismological institutions are defined by station number, spacing and data flow. Published locations retain model-dependent uncertainty.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 3 for the P-S speed and medium contrast stated with particle motion; 3 for the S-P interval derived or at least justified as proportional to distance; 2 for the three-station requirement explained as the consequence of distance-without-direction; 1 for focal depth included in the solution; 1 for the velocity-model uncertainty conceded. Deduct 2 for claiming a single station can locate an event and 2 for confusing early warning with prediction.

## Lesson 10 — Measuring earthquakes: magnitude, intensity and the move from Richter to moment magnitude

Progress: 10 / 16  |  Stage: Core  |  Subtopic: The logarithmic magnitude relation derived, the saturation problem that retired the local scale, and intensity as a separate and place-specific quantity

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §1 and §8.2 (local magnitude as logarithmic, 10 times amplitude and about 31.6 times energy; large modern events reported by **moment magnitude (Mw)**; *"'Richter' is a popular but imprecise generic label"*; the magnitude-against-intensity trap; the date/status control requiring issuing agency, observation date and version); Advanced owner §1 (the same correction, applied to Indian reporting); Majid Husain, *Indian & World Geography*, printed p.2.19 (the Richter scale as open-ended and logarithmic, 10-fold amplitude and 31.5-fold energy; the Mercalli scale as intensity).
CA search: "National Center for Seismology magnitude depth reporting convention Indian earthquakes 2026" — run for this lesson.
CA found: None in last 6 months. The institutional docket verified for this edition is carried at Lesson 9 and is not re-used here to pad a checklist; the dated Indian events named below are taken from the canonical owner's own record, not from a current fetch.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

```text
 (A) THE MAGNITUDE-ENERGY LADDER — why one unit is not "a bit more"

  M     recorded wave      energy released        verbal sense
        AMPLITUDE          (relative)
  ---   ---------------    -----------------      ----------------------------
  4.0   x1                 x1                     felt; rarely damaging
  5.0   x10                x~31.6                 damaging to weak structures
  6.0   x100               x~1,000                destructive over a small area
  7.0   x1,000             x~31,600               major
  8.0   x10,000            x~1,000,000            great
  9.0   x100,000           x~31,600,000           among the largest recorded
        |                  |
        |                  +-- DERIVATION: energy scales as amplitude^1.5,
        |                      so one magnitude unit = 10^1.5 = sqrt(1000)
        |                      = 31.62...  (quoted as "31.5" and "about 31.6"
        |                      in different sources - SAME relation, rounded)
        +-- the scale is LOGARITHMIC and OPEN-ENDED: no fixed upper bound,
            and no zero below which nothing exists

 (B) ISOSEISMAL SKETCH — intensity is a FIELD, magnitude is a NUMBER

        soft alluvium                        hard rock
      ....VIII....                            ..VI..
    ..VII..........                        ..VII....
  ..VI....(X)........                    ..VIII..
    ..V......  ^                       ..(X)..        <- SAME EVENT,
      ..IV....  \                    ..VII..             SAME MAGNITUDE
          |      \ epicentre              |
          |       \__ isoseismals are STRETCHED along the soft ground
          |           and COMPRESSED over rock
          v
   ONE EARTHQUAKE HAS ONE MAGNITUDE AND MANY INTENSITIES.
   Intensity at a place depends on distance, depth, SOIL and CONSTRUCTION.
```

The ladder is the arithmetic; the isoseismal sketch is the geography. Keeping them apart is the single most reliable mark in any earthquake answer.

### 1. The logarithmic relation, derived rather than asserted

✅ Majid states the scale's two properties: *"The Richter Scale is open ended and logarithmic, i.e. each whole number on the scale represents a 10-fold increase in the measured wave amplitude"*, and *"translated into energy, each whole number demonstrates a 31.5-fold increase in the amount of energy released."* ✅ The Core owner states the same relation as *"10 times recorded wave amplitude and about 31.6 times energy."*

⚠️ **The two figures are not a disagreement; they are two roundings of one number, and saying so is worth a mark.** Energy scales approximately as amplitude raised to the power of one and a half, so one magnitude unit multiplies energy by **10 raised to the power 1.5**, which is the square root of a thousand, approximately **31.62**. ❗ Quoting "31.5" or "about 31.6" is therefore equally defensible; quoting "about 32 times, because energy goes as the three-halves power of amplitude" is better than either, because it shows the relation rather than the rounding.

❗ **The consequence candidates get wrong.** ⚠️ A one-unit rise is **not** a doubling. **Two** units multiply amplitude by a hundred and energy by about a thousand. **Three** units multiply energy by about thirty-one thousand. ⚠️ This is why the global earthquake energy budget is dominated by a handful of the very largest events, and why a catalogue containing hundreds of thousands of small events (Lesson 8) tells you almost nothing about the energy released in a year.

⚠️ **"Open ended" matters too.** There is no design maximum on the scale; the practical upper limit is set by **how much fault area can rupture in one event**, which is a property of the Earth rather than of the scale.

### 2. Why the local magnitude had to be replaced — the mandatory correction

❗ **This is the obsolete-terminology correction that this topic is required to make explicitly, and it must be made as a correction rather than as a silent substitution.**

⚠️ The scale popularly called "Richter" is properly the **local magnitude**, devised for a particular instrument, a particular region and a limited distance range. It works well for moderate earthquakes. It fails for great ones, and the failure has a name: **saturation**.

```text
   WHY A LOCAL MAGNITUDE SATURATES

   local magnitude is read from the AMPLITUDE of a particular
   wave at a particular PERIOD on a particular instrument
            |
            v
   a GREAT earthquake ruptures a very long fault over TENS OF SECONDS
   to several MINUTES, radiating most of its energy at LONG periods
            |
            v
   the short-period amplitude the scale measures STOPS GROWING
   even though the earthquake keeps getting bigger
            |
            v
   SATURATION: two events of very different true size return almost
   the SAME local magnitude  ==>  the scale under-reports great events

   THE REPLACEMENT: MOMENT MAGNITUDE (Mw), derived from SEISMIC MOMENT
         seismic moment = rigidity x RUPTURE AREA x average SLIP
            |
            v
   a PHYSICAL quantity, not an instrument reading; it does NOT saturate,
   and it is what great modern earthquakes are reported in
```

⚠️ The Core owner states the operative position: *"Large modern earthquakes are usually reported by **moment magnitude (Mw)**"*, and *"local magnitude (often popularly called the Richter scale)"* is the historical instrument. ⚠️ The Advanced owner repeats it for Indian reporting: *"Earthquake size is now commonly reported as **moment magnitude (Mw)**; 'Richter' is a popular but imprecise generic label."*

❗ **How to handle the word "Richter" in an answer.** ⚠️ Do not pretend it does not exist and do not treat it as simply wrong. **Name it as a historical scale** — the local magnitude, a real and important measurement — and **name its present status**, which is that of a **popular generic label** used loosely in media reporting for any magnitude figure. ❗ An answer that writes *"reported at magnitude 7.6 — media reports usually say 'on the Richter scale', but great events are reported in moment magnitude, which does not saturate"* has demonstrated precision in half a line. ⚠️ Both local sources available to this edition report great historical earthquakes *"on the Richter scale"*; that is a **book-era usage**, and it is named as such rather than reproduced uncritically.

⚠️ **A second consequence of the same point, developed at Lesson 12.** Because magnitude for a great event is **computed** from a physical model rather than read off an instrument, it is **revised** as more data arrive. ❗ A published magnitude is therefore an **estimate with a date and an issuing agency attached**, and the Core owner's date-and-status control requires exactly that: *"issuing agency, observation/publication date and version."*

### 3. Intensity — a different quantity, not a different scale for the same thing

✅ Majid: *"Earthquake magnitude is estimated by the Richter Scale, while intensity of the earthquake is described by the Mercalli Scale."* ✅ The Core owner: **magnitude** is *"a single, instrument-derived measure of energy released"*; **intensity** *"varies from place to place with distance, depth, soil and construction quality."*

| | **Magnitude** | **Intensity** |
|---|---|---|
| What it measures | The **size of the event** — energy radiated | The **effect at a place** — what was felt and damaged |
| How many values per earthquake | **One** | **Many** — one for every location |
| How obtained | Instrumentally, from the wave record or from seismic moment | By **observation** of felt effects and damage |
| Scale in use | Local magnitude historically; **moment magnitude (Mw)** for great events | **Modified Mercalli (MM)**; **MSK** widely used in India |
| Nature of the scale | Continuous, open-ended, logarithmic | **Ordinal**, in Roman numerals, bounded in practice |
| Depends on soil and buildings? | **No** | **Yes, decisively** |
| Mapped as | A number | **Isoseismals** — contours of equal intensity |

❗ **The trap, in the Core owner's own words:** *"A moderate-magnitude shallow earthquake on soft alluvium can be far more damaging than a larger deep one."* ⚠️ That single sentence is the most transferable statement in this lesson, because it explains why magnitude alone cannot rank disasters, and it is the direct bridge to Lesson 13.

⚠️ **Isoseismals are a geographical object and should be treated as one.** They are contours of equal intensity drawn from observed effects; they are **not** concentric circles around the epicentre, and their departure from circularity is the data. ⚠️ Elongation along a river valley or an alluvial basin records **site amplification**; compression over a rock outcrop records the opposite; offsets record **directivity** of the rupture. ❗ An isoseismal map is therefore a map of **ground and building response**, and it is the empirical foundation of microzonation at Lesson 15.

⚠️ **Peak ground acceleration (PGA)** sits between the two columns and is worth naming because the design code at Lesson 15 is written in it. ⚠️ It is an **instrumental measure of the ground motion at a site**, so it is site-specific like intensity but objective like magnitude — which is why modern zonation expresses hazard in acceleration terms rather than in felt-intensity terms. ❗ **This file quotes no PGA value for any Indian zone**, since that is a code provision rather than a geographical fact.

### 4. 🇮🇳 India hook — how Indian events are reported, and why the wording matters

🇮🇳 ✅ The Core owner records the Indian reporting convention and attaches dated examples: *"earthquake magnitude (Richter) vs intensity (Mercalli) and focus/epicentre are the exact terms the National Center for Seismology uses to report"* Indian events, with the owner's own dated instances being **back-to-back tremors at Jhajjar, Haryana — M 4.4 on 10 July 2025 and M 3.7 on 11 July 2025 — felt across Delhi-NCR**, and a **M 7.1 event near the Nepal-Tibet border on 7 January 2025**, felt across north India.

⚠️ **Read those two cases together and the whole lesson is demonstrated in Indian terms.** The Jhajjar events are **small in magnitude** and were **widely felt** because they were shallow and close to a very large population on alluvium; the Nepal-Tibet event is **far larger in magnitude** and was felt as a tremor in north India because of distance. ❗ **Magnitude ranked the two events one way; intensity in Delhi ranked them another**, which is precisely why both quantities exist.

❗ **Limits, and they are important.** ⚠️ The dated events above are the **canonical owner's record**, not a current fetch, and they are more than six months old at the time of this edition; they are used as **illustrations of reporting convention**, not as current affairs. ❗ **This edition asserts no depth, no intensity value, no damage figure and no casualty figure for either event.** ❗ And the Core owner's own control applies: *"no single tremor validates a deterministic recurrence or zone change"* — so neither event is used to argue anything about Indian zonation.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| | Local magnitude ("Richter") | Moment magnitude (Mw) | Intensity (MM / MSK) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Basis | Amplitude at a fixed period on a specific instrument | **Seismic moment** — rigidity × rupture area × average slip | Observed effects on people, objects, structures and ground |
| Behaves well for | Moderate, regional earthquakes | **All sizes, including great events** | Any event, wherever it was felt |
| Fails because | **Saturates** for great earthquakes | Requires modelling, so it is **revised** | **Subjective and construction-dependent** |
| Values per event | One | One | Many |
| Present status | Historical scale; **popular generic label** | **The reporting standard for great events** | Essential for damage mapping and zonation |

**Strongest objection.** This is a vocabulary quibble. Everyone understands what "7.6 on the Richter scale" means, so correcting it is pedantry.

**Reply.** The correction is not about the word; it is about a **systematic measurement error**. ❗ A saturating scale **under-reports precisely the events that matter most** — the great ones — so the historical catalogue compiled on it is not merely imprecisely labelled, it is **biased at the top end**. ⚠️ Any argument about recurrence, about the comparative size of historical earthquakes, or about whether a region has experienced a great event therefore depends on which magnitude was used and when. ⚠️ And the second half of the correction is substantive too: treating magnitude as the measure of a disaster conceals the fact that **soil, depth and construction determine loss**, which is the error that produces an answer about earthquakes and not about earthquake risk.

⚠️ **Residual.** Moment magnitude itself is a **computed estimate** with uncertainty, and different agencies may publish different values for the same event at the same time. ❗ This file therefore attaches **no magnitude figure to any event that is not quoted from a named source with its attribution**, and treats all such figures as **reported estimates**, not settled quantities.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** "important Geophysical phenomena such as earthquakes" (Mains GS-I, direct ownership); the GS-III disaster clause in a support capacity, since risk statements depend on the measurement convention.
- **Verified linkage:** serves the precision of **all four owned Mains demands** — the 2018, 2020, 2021 and 2025 answers each contain at least one magnitude or intensity statement — and underpins the 2023 objective route on wave properties.
- **Probable framing:** this lesson almost never supplies a whole answer. It supplies **one disciplined sentence** inside several, and the discipline is what is marked.
- **Traps:** saying one magnitude unit doubles the energy; treating Richter and Mercalli as alternative scales for the same quantity; ranking disasters by magnitude; quoting a magnitude without an agency or a date; drawing isoseismals as circles; asserting a PGA value for an Indian zone.
- **Answer use:** *"Magnitude is one number for the event; intensity is a field of values over the ground. A moderate shallow earthquake on soft alluvium can produce higher intensities, and far greater loss, than a larger deep one — which is why hazard maps are drawn in intensity or acceleration terms, not in magnitude."*

Revision notes:

- ✅ **Logarithmic and open-ended:** each whole number = **×10 amplitude**; ✅ **×31.5** (Majid) or ⚠️ **×about 31.6** (owner) energy. ⚠️ **Same relation**: energy ∝ amplitude^1.5, so one unit = 10^1.5 = **√1000 ≈ 31.62**.
- ❗ **One unit is not a doubling.** Two units ≈ **×1,000 energy**; three ≈ **×31,600**. ⚠️ A few great events dominate the annual energy budget.
- ❗ **Mandatory correction:** the popular "Richter" scale is the **local magnitude**, built for one instrument, one region and a limited distance range, and it **SATURATES** for great earthquakes — short-period amplitude stops growing while the event keeps growing.
- ⚠️ **Moment magnitude (Mw)** replaces it: derived from **seismic moment = rigidity × rupture area × average slip**; **physical, not instrumental; does not saturate**; the reporting standard for great events.
- ⚠️ Handle "Richter" as a **named historical scale** and a **popular generic media label** — not as simply wrong, not as current practice. ⚠️ Both local books report great events *"on the Richter scale"* — **book-era usage**.
- ❗ Magnitude for a great event is **computed and therefore revised**; quote it with **issuing agency, date and version**.
- ✅ **Magnitude = size of the event, one value, instrumental. Intensity = effect at a place, many values, observational.** ✅ **Mercalli describes intensity**; **MM** and **MSK** are the scales, Roman numerals, **ordinal**.
- ❗ ✅ **"A moderate-magnitude shallow earthquake on soft alluvium can be far more damaging than a larger deep one."** The most transferable sentence in the lesson.
- ⚠️ **Isoseismals = contours of equal intensity.** ❗ **Not circles.** Elongation along alluvium records **site amplification**; offsets record rupture **directivity**. They are the empirical basis of **microzonation**.
- ⚠️ **Peak ground acceleration** is site-specific like intensity but **instrumental like magnitude** — which is why modern codes are written in acceleration. ❗ **No PGA value quoted here.**
- 🇮🇳 ✅ Owner-recorded Indian instances of the reporting convention: **Jhajjar, Haryana, M 4.4 on 10 July 2025 and M 3.7 on 11 July 2025**, felt across Delhi-NCR; **M 7.1 near the Nepal-Tibet border, 7 January 2025**, felt across north India. ❗ **No depth, intensity, damage or casualty figure asserted**; ❗ **no single tremor validates a recurrence or a zone change**.
- ⚠️ The Indian pair demonstrates the whole lesson: **small magnitude widely felt** (shallow, close, alluvium, dense population) against **large magnitude felt only as a tremor** (distant).

### Concept check

**Question:** Two earthquakes occur in the same year. Event A is reported at magnitude 6.1 and causes more than ten thousand deaths; event B is reported at magnitude 8.0 and causes seventeen injuries. A newspaper concludes that magnitude is "a meaningless measure". Assess the conclusion, and state what the two events jointly establish.

**Model answer:** The conclusion is wrong, but the observation behind it is correct. Magnitude is a measure of **the size of the event**: it is a single instrumental or model-derived number quantifying the energy radiated, and on that measure event B is vastly larger — a difference of 1.9 units corresponds to roughly a hundred times the amplitude and of the order of a thousand times the energy. What magnitude does **not** measure is loss, because loss is produced by **intensity at inhabited places**, which varies with distance, focal depth, soil condition and construction quality. Event A's toll is consistent with a shallow focus beneath or near a dense population standing on unconsolidated sediment in poorly engineered buildings; event B's near-absence of casualties is consistent with distance from population, greater depth, firm ground, or seismically engineered construction, or some combination. The two events jointly establish that **magnitude and intensity are different quantities that must both be stated**, and more pointedly that **hazard is not disaster**: the physical event is only one of four variables, the others being exposure, vulnerability and site condition. The newspaper's error is to demand of one measure a job that requires four.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not respond to this contrast by concluding that intensity is the "real" measure and magnitude a technicality. Magnitude is indispensable precisely because it is independent of where people happen to live: it is what makes two earthquakes in different countries comparable at all, and it is what a recurrence estimate is built from.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "The replacement of the Richter scale by moment magnitude was a correction of measurement, not of terminology." Explain. *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** The local magnitude popularly called Richter is read from wave amplitude at a fixed period on a specified instrument, and is logarithmic: each unit represents ten times the amplitude and roughly thirty-two times the energy, energy varying as the three-halves power of amplitude. The scale performs well for moderate events. It fails for great ones because a long rupture radiates predominantly at long periods over tens of seconds or minutes, so the short-period amplitude it measures ceases to grow while the earthquake grows. This is saturation, and its effect is systematic: the largest events are under-reported, and any catalogue built on it is biased at its upper end. Moment magnitude derives instead from seismic moment — rigidity by rupture area by average slip — a physical quantity that does not saturate. The change therefore repaired a measurement defect; the residual is that moment magnitude is itself a revisable estimate.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 3 for saturation explained by the long-period radiation of long ruptures, not merely named; 3 for seismic moment defined by its three factors and identified as physical rather than instrumental; 2 for the logarithmic relation stated with the energy exponent; 1 for the catalogue-bias consequence; 1 for the revisability of moment magnitude conceded. Deduct 2 for treating the change as a naming convention and 2 for stating that one magnitude unit doubles the energy.

## Lesson 11 — Tsunami generation: why only some undersea earthquakes make waves

Progress: 11 / 16  |  Stage: Core  |  Subtopic: The vertical displacement condition, the magnitude and depth thresholds, the four triggers, and why a tsunami is a train rather than a wave

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner, the tsunami block in full (the definition attributed to Majid Husain; sudden vertical displacement of the ocean floor; chief cause submarine thrust faulting at subduction zones; volcanic eruption and submarine landslide as the other causes; the "not a tidal wave" trap); Advanced owner §"2004 Indian Ocean Tsunami"; D.R. Khullar, *India: A Comprehensive Geography*, printed pp.226-229 (the etymology and the High Powered Committee definition; the generation conditions; the four causes; Fig. 8.3's three-panel triggering diagram); Majid Husain, *Indian & World Geography*, printed pp.4.13-4.14 (low steepness; period of five to six minutes; trench association; the 1946 Aleutian, 1703 Awa, 1883 Krakatoa and 1755 Lisbon cases); G.C. Leong, local-scan p.26 (Krakatau 1883 and Anak Krakatau).
CA search: "tsunami generation threshold submarine earthquake official advisory Indian Ocean 2026" — run for this lesson.
CA found: None in last 6 months. The verified institutional docket for the Indian warning system is carried at Lesson 12, where the system belongs; it is not duplicated here to fill a checklist.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

```text
  "DOES THIS UNDERSEA EARTHQUAKE MAKE A TSUNAMI?" — a decision tree

  START: an earthquake occurs beneath the sea
     |
     +--> Is the FOCUS SHALLOW?  (most destructive cases: LESS than 50 km)
     |        |
     |        +-- NO --> the rupture does not reach near enough to the sea
     |        |          floor to deform it. --> USUALLY NO TSUNAMI
     |        v YES
     +--> Is the event LARGE?  (most destructive cases: M GREATER than 7.5)
     |        |
     |        +-- NO --> displaced volume too small to radiate a long wave
     |        |          of destructive amplitude. --> USUALLY NO TSUNAMI
     |        v YES
     +--> Is the sea-floor motion VERTICAL?
     |        |
     |        +-- NO, it is HORIZONTAL (strike-slip) --> the water column is
     |        |      pushed SIDEWAYS, not LIFTED. Water flows around the
     |        |      displacement. --> USUALLY NO TSUNAMI  <<== THE KEY CLAUSE
     |        v YES (thrust faulting: the sea floor is LIFTED or DROPPED)
     +--> Is the water column DEEP enough, over a LARGE enough area?
              |            (ruptures run from a few km to over 1,000 km)
              +-- NO --> small, local wave only
              v YES
       ===> THE WHOLE WATER COLUMN IS DISPLACED VERTICALLY
            potential energy of a raised/lowered water mass
                        |
                        v
            it collapses and radiates OUTWARD as a train of
            VERY LONG WAVELENGTH, VERY LOW AMPLITUDE waves
                        |
                        v
            ==> A TSUNAMI: not one wave, A SERIES.
                Period of the order of MINUTES, not seconds.

  THREE OTHER TRIGGERS DO THE SAME JOB BY OTHER MEANS:
    submarine LANDSLIDE  | volcanic ERUPTION or CALDERA COLLAPSE
    | bolide impact (meteorite / asteroid)
```

A decision tree, not a definition. Every "no" branch is a fact an examiner can test, and the third branch — horizontal motion producing nothing — is the one that distinguishes a competent answer from an excellent one.

### 1. What a tsunami is, and the three things it is not

✅ Majid Husain's definition is the one the Core owner adopts: *"The seismic waves, travelling through the ocean and sea water, result into high sea waves which are known as tsunamis"*, the word being **Japanese**, *"universally adopted to describe the seismic sea waves"*. ✅ Khullar supplies the etymology precisely — ***tsu*** meaning harbour and ***nami*** meaning wave — and records the definition given in the **Report of the High Powered Committee on Disaster Management (2001)**.

❗ **Three negations do more work than the definition.**

- ❗ **A tsunami is not a tidal wave.** ⚠️ The Core owner states the trap in terms: *"A tsunami is a **seismic sea wave**, NOT a 'tidal wave' — tides have nothing to do with it."* Tides are driven by gravitational forcing from the Moon and the Sun; a tsunami is driven by a displacement of the sea floor.
- ❗ **A tsunami is not a wind wave.** ⚠️ Wind waves are generated at the sea surface by shear stress and involve motion in the upper part of the water column only; a tsunami involves **the entire water column from sea bed to surface**, which is why its energy is so enormous and why it is unaffected by local weather.
- ❗ **A tsunami is not a single wave.** ✅ Khullar is explicit that it is **a series**, and Majid's figure draws a numbered train. ⚠️ The practical consequence is the one that kills: **the first wave is often not the largest**, and the interval between waves is of the order of minutes to tens of minutes, so a coastline that appears to have survived may not have.

### 2. The generation conditions, stated as thresholds

✅ Khullar's generation account is the most precise statement available to this edition and it is the core of the 2025 demand's first half.

| Condition | ✅ As recorded | ⚠️ Why it is necessary |
|---|---|---|
| **Focal depth** | Most destructive tsunamis arise from earthquakes **shallower than 50 km** | A deep rupture dissipates into the overlying rock and does not deform the sea floor appreciably |
| **Magnitude** | Most destructive tsunamis arise from events of **M greater than 7.5** | The displaced volume scales with rupture area and slip; small events displace too little water to radiate a destructive long wave |
| **Rupture extent** | Sea-floor deformation extends from **a few kilometres to more than 1,000 km** | A longer source radiates a longer-crested wave that spreads its energy less with distance |
| **Direction of motion** | ❗ **Vertical displacement of the whole water column is required**; **horizontal sea-floor movement usually generates no tsunami** | Water is effectively incompressible but freely mobile: lift it and you store potential energy; push it sideways and it flows around the obstruction |
| **Not automatic** | ❗ **Not every undersea earthquake produces a tsunami** | All four conditions must be met together |

❗ **The fourth row is the single highest-value fact in the tsunami arc.** ⚠️ It explains why a **thrust fault at a subduction zone** — where the overriding plate is driven up or snaps back upward — is the dominant tsunami source, while a **strike-slip fault** of the same magnitude beneath the same depth of water commonly produces nothing. ⚠️ It also explains the apparent anomaly that an undersea earthquake can be felt on a coast without any wave following.

### 3. The four triggers, with the three-panel reconstruction

✅ Khullar's figure sets out the triggering mechanisms side by side; reconstructed as an original visual, with the fourth trigger added from his own list:

```text
  TRIGGER 1: SUBMARINE EARTHQUAKE (the dominant case)
     sea surface ~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~^^^^^^~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~
                                 | water column LIFTED
     sea floor ================__/====================
                        thrust fault: overriding plate
                        snaps UPWARD  ==> VERTICAL displacement

  TRIGGER 2: SUBMARINE LANDSLIDE
     sea surface ~~~~~~~~~~~~^^^^~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~
                              ||  water DROPS into the void
     slope ====\\             ||        and PILES at the toe
                \\__  slide __vv__
                    \\========= ==> displacement by MASS MOVEMENT
                     (often triggered BY an earthquake: a secondary path)

  TRIGGER 3: VOLCANIC ERUPTION / CALDERA COLLAPSE
     sea surface ~~~~~~~~~~~^^^^^^^^~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~
                   /\         ||
                  /  \  BOOM  ||  flank collapse, caldera subsidence,
     ===========/ || \========vv  or a pyroclastic current entering the sea
                  ||          ==> displacement by SUDDEN VOLUME CHANGE
                  Krakatau, August 1883 is the type-case

  TRIGGER 4: BOLIDE IMPACT (meteorite / asteroid)
     sea surface ~~~~~~~~~\  o  /~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~
                           \ | /   cavity excavated and collapsing
     ====================== \|/ ==> rare; recorded as a cause
```

⚠️ **The second trigger deserves a sentence of its own because it is a chain.** A submarine landslide is frequently **triggered by an earthquake**, so an event can generate a tsunami **twice over**: directly by sea-floor displacement, and indirectly by the slide it sets off. ❗ This is why a tsunami can be larger than the generating earthquake's magnitude alone would predict, and why local run-up can be extreme near a slide source.

⚠️ **The third trigger is where volcanism rejoins this topic.** ✅ Leong's Krakatau account supplies the mechanism and the scale: in **August 1883**, in the **Sunda Strait**, **two-thirds of the island collapsed into a submarine caldera** and the resulting waves exceeded **100 ft**. ✅ Majid records the same event as generating **35-metre waves** that **destroyed 163 villages** and killed **more than 36,000 people**. ❗ ⚠️ **The two sources give different wave heights for the same event** — which is itself the lesson of Lesson 10's measurement discipline, and this edition reports both **with their attributions** rather than selecting one.

### 4. Where tsunamis are generated — the geography of the source

✅ Majid records the distribution: tsunamis *"occur in or around the oceanic trenches"* and are *"more frequent in the Pacific ocean"*, though *"the Atlantic ocean and the Indian ocean are not free"* from them. ⚠️ That is Lesson 6's architecture seen from the sea: **the trench marks the plate interface, the shallow part of that interface is where megathrust rupture occurs, and it lies beneath deep water** — the three conditions of §2 satisfied simultaneously by one structure.

✅ **The named generation cases in the sources**, each with its trigger:

| ✅ Event | ✅ Date | ✅ Trigger as recorded |
|---|---|---|
| **Awa, Japan** | **1703** | Seismic sea waves; **one lakh deaths** |
| **Lisbon** | **1755** | The Lisbon earthquake; waves **over 15 metres**; **over 60,000 killed** |
| **Krakatoa, Sunda Strait** | **August 1883** | **Volcanic**: caldera collapse; **35-metre waves**, **163 villages**, **over 36,000 dead** |
| **Aleutian Trench** | **1 April 1946** | A **fracture along the trench**; the tsunami *"quickly engulfed the Scotch-Cap lighthouse in Unimak Island"*, destroying it and killing **five coastguards** |
| **Banda Aceh, Sumatra** | **26 December 2004** | **Sunda Trench** megathrust; **over two lakh deaths** in neighbouring countries |
| **Japan** | **11 March 2011** | Japan Trench megathrust; **more than 25,000 killed** in and around Sendai; ravaged the **Fukushima** nuclear plant |

❗ **A dating correction, declared.** ⚠️ One local source's tabulated earthquake list dates the Banda Aceh event to **January 2004**, while the same book's running tsunami text correctly gives **26 December 2004**. ❗ **The table is not used anywhere in this edition**; the running text is, and **26 December 2004** is the date taught.

### 5. The physical signature at sea — why it passes unnoticed

✅ Majid describes the open-ocean behaviour exactly: once generated, a tsunami's **steepness — the ratio of height to length — is extremely low**, and *"this lack of steepness, combined with wave's very long period (5 to 6 minutes), enables it to pass unnoticed beneath ship at sea."*

⚠️ **This is the most counter-intuitive fact in the topic and it is worth stating as a paradox and then resolving it.** A wave carrying enough energy to devastate a continental coastline is **invisible** in deep water: an amplitude of less than a metre spread over a wavelength of hundreds of kilometres produces a slope so slight that a vessel registers only a slow, imperceptible rise and fall. ❗ The energy is not in the height; it is in **the volume of water set in motion through the entire depth of the column**. ⚠️ Lesson 12 shows what happens when that column is forced into shallow water, and the derivation there is what makes the paradox quantitative.

### 6. 🇮🇳 India hook — the Sunda Trench and its Andaman segment as India's tsunamigenic source

🇮🇳 India's tsunami hazard has a specific, named, mappable source, and it is the same structure Lesson 6 described. ⚠️ The **Sunda Trench** marks the convergence of the Indian-Australian lithosphere with the Southeast Asian margin; its northern continuation runs past Sumatra and **through the Andaman segment into the Andaman Sea**, which is why the island arc exists at all.

✅ The 2004 source geography, as recorded by Khullar: the epicentre lay **off Sumatra at 3.5° N, 95° E**, at the **tri-junction of the Indian, Australian and Burmese plates**; there was about **15 m of vertical slip** along a rupture of the order of **1,000 km**, extending **north to the Andaman and Nicobar Islands**. ❗ **Every one of §2's four conditions is visible in that sentence**: shallow subduction interface, very large magnitude, a rupture of order one thousand kilometres, and — decisively — **vertical slip of about fifteen metres**.

⚠️ **The structural consequence for India is permanent and is the examinable point.** Because the rupture can extend **into the Andaman segment**, India's hazard is not only **distant-source** — a wave arriving from Sumatra after a travel time of hours — but also **near-source**, where a rupture within or adjacent to the Andaman and Nicobar group would leave almost no warning interval at all. ❗ That distinction between a **near-field** and a **far-field** source is what makes the warning system of Lesson 12 a geographical problem rather than merely an institutional one.

❗ **Limits.** ⚠️ The 2004 slip and rupture-length figures are **as recorded in a named source** and are estimates subject to revision. ❗ **No recurrence interval, no probability and no maximum credible magnitude is asserted for the Andaman segment**, because none is carried by any source available to this edition — and because, per Lesson 8, such a claim would be a prediction rather than a description.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| | Wind wave | Tide | Tsunami |
|---|---|---|---|
| Driving force | Wind shear on the sea surface | Gravitational attraction of Moon and Sun | **Sudden vertical displacement of the sea floor** |
| Depth of motion | Upper part of the water column | Whole column, but gently and periodically | **Whole column, suddenly** |
| Period | Seconds | About twelve hours | ✅ **Minutes — five to six** |
| Wavelength | Tens to hundreds of metres | Ocean-basin scale | **Hundreds of kilometres** |
| Visibility at sea | Obvious | Obvious at the coast | ✅ **Passes unnoticed beneath a ship** |
| Number of events | Continuous | Regular and predictable | ✅ **A series of waves**, irregular, the first not necessarily the largest |

**Strongest objection.** Generation is a technicality. The examiner asked what tsunamis are, how and where they are formed, and what their consequences are — so the marks must be in the consequences.

**Reply.** The 2025 demand's own wording refutes this. ⚠️ *"How and where are they formed"* is **two of the three clauses**, and "how" cannot be answered by naming submarine earthquakes, because that answers "what triggers them", not "how they are formed". ❗ **The mechanism is the vertical displacement of the entire water column and the conversion of its potential energy into a long-wavelength wave train**, and the negative clause — that **horizontal sea-floor motion usually produces none** — is the clearest available demonstration that the candidate has understood the mechanism rather than memorised the trigger. ⚠️ **Where** is then answered structurally, by trench geography, rather than by a list of countries.

⚠️ **Residual.** The magnitude and depth thresholds in §2 are **empirical generalisations about destructive events**, not physical cut-offs: smaller or deeper events have produced local tsunamis, particularly where a landslide intervened. ❗ Treat them as **typical conditions**, write them as such, and the qualification itself earns a mark.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** "important Geophysical phenomena such as... Tsunami" (Mains GS-I, direct ownership). 🔁 The disaster-management owner holds warning governance and response doctrine; **physical-process ownership remains with Geography**, and this lesson is its first half.
- **Verified linkage:** **carries the first half of the 2025 General Studies Paper I demand — tsunamis, their formation, 10 marks, about 150 words.** The consequences half is Lesson 12.
- **Probable framing:** in a 150-word answer, generation deserves roughly half the space: definition in one clause, the four conditions with the vertical-displacement clause explicit, the four triggers in a single sentence, and the train-not-a-wave point.
- **Traps:** calling it a tidal wave; writing "submarine earthquake" as the mechanism rather than as the trigger; omitting the horizontal-motion exclusion; treating the first wave as the largest; implying that every undersea earthquake produces one; asserting a recurrence interval for any source region.
- **Answer use:** *"A tsunami forms only where the whole water column is displaced vertically — typically by thrust faulting at a shallow focus, under 50 km, in an event larger than magnitude 7.5; horizontal sea-floor motion of the same size usually generates none, which is why strike-slip earthquakes beneath deep water are seldom tsunamigenic."*

Revision notes:

- ✅ **Definition:** *"seismic waves travelling through the ocean... result into high sea waves which are known as tsunamis"*; **Japanese**, ✅ ***tsu*** = harbour + ***nami*** = wave; ✅ defined in the **Report of the High Powered Committee on Disaster Management (2001)**.
- ❗ **Three negations:** **not a tidal wave** (tides are gravitational) · **not a wind wave** (wind moves only the upper column) · ✅ **not a single wave — a series**, and **the first is often not the largest**.
- ✅ **Generation conditions:** focal depth **< 50 km** · **M > 7.5** · sea-floor deformation from **a few km to over 1,000 km** · ❗ **vertical displacement of the whole water column REQUIRED** · ❗ **horizontal sea-floor motion usually generates NONE** · ❗ **not every undersea earthquake makes one**.
- ⚠️ Mechanism: water is **mobile but incompressible** — **lift** it and you store potential energy; **push it sideways** and it flows around the displacement.
- ⚠️ **Thrust faulting at subduction zones** is therefore the dominant source; **strike-slip** of equal magnitude usually is not.
- **Four triggers:** **submarine earthquake** · **submarine landslide** (often **earthquake-triggered** — a double path) · **volcanic eruption / caldera collapse** · **bolide impact**.
- ✅ **Krakatoa, August 1883** — volcanic trigger; ✅ **two-thirds of the island collapsed** into a submarine caldera; ✅ waves **> 100 ft** (Leong) and ✅ **35 m**, **163 villages**, **> 36,000 dead** (Majid). ❗ **Sources differ on height; both are reported with attribution.**
- ✅ **Distribution:** *"in or around the oceanic trenches"*, **most frequent in the Pacific**; the Atlantic and Indian Oceans **not free** of them.
- ✅ **Named cases:** **Awa 1703** (one lakh) · **Lisbon 1755** (> 15 m, > 60,000) · **Krakatoa 1883** · **Aleutian Trench, 1 April 1946** — **Scotch Cap lighthouse, Unimak Island, destroyed, five coastguards killed** · **Banda Aceh, 26 December 2004** · **Japan, 11 March 2011** (> 25,000; Fukushima).
- ❗ **Declared correction:** one source's **table** dates Banda Aceh to **January 2004**; its running text correctly gives **26 December 2004**. **The table is not used; the correct date is taught.**
- ✅ **At sea:** steepness **extremely low**; **period 5 to 6 minutes**; *"passes unnoticed beneath ship at sea"*. ⚠️ The energy is in the **volume of the whole column**, not in the height.
- 🇮🇳 ✅ **2004 source:** epicentre **off Sumatra at 3.5° N, 95° E**, at the **tri-junction of the Indian, Australian and Burmese plates**; ~**15 m vertical slip** along a ~**1,000 km** rupture extending **north to the Andaman and Nicobar Islands**.
- 🇮🇳 ⚠️ **The Sunda Trench's Andaman segment makes India both a FAR-FIELD and a NEAR-FIELD tsunami target** — which is why warning time is a geographical variable. ❗ **No recurrence interval or maximum credible magnitude asserted.**
- ⚠️ ❗ The thresholds are **empirical generalisations about destructive events**, not physical cut-offs.

### Concept check

**Question:** Two submarine earthquakes of magnitude 7.8 occur on the same day at focal depths of 20 km beneath water 4,000 m deep. The first is a thrust event on a subduction interface; the second is a strike-slip event on a transform fault. Only the first generates a destructive tsunami. Explain why, and state the circumstance in which the second could nevertheless produce one.

**Model answer:** The difference lies in the **direction of sea-floor motion**, not in the size, the depth or the water. A tsunami is generated by the **vertical displacement of the entire water column**: when a thrust rupture drives the overriding plate upward, it lifts the whole thickness of water above it, creating a mass of raised water whose potential energy collapses and radiates outward as a train of very long, very low-amplitude waves. A strike-slip rupture moves the sea floor **horizontally**. Because water is mobile, it simply flows around a lateral displacement rather than being raised, so almost no potential energy is stored and horizontal sea-floor movement usually generates no tsunami — which is precisely why magnitude and depth alone are insufficient criteria. The second event could still produce one by an **indirect path**: strong shaking on a steep submarine slope can trigger a **submarine landslide**, and the slide displaces water vertically by removing mass from one place and piling it at another. Such landslide-generated tsunamis are typically more localised than megathrust tsunamis but can produce very high local run-up, because the source is close to the shore.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not use magnitude as a sufficient test for tsunami potential. The threshold conditions — shallow focus, large magnitude, extensive rupture — are necessary but not sufficient, and the sufficient condition is the geometry of the displacement. An answer that omits the vertical-displacement clause has named the trigger without describing the mechanism.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** Explain how and where tsunamis are formed, distinguishing the trigger from the mechanism. *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** The mechanism is the sudden vertical displacement of the entire water column. A rupture that lifts or drops the sea floor raises a mass of water whose potential energy collapses and radiates outward as a train of waves of very long wavelength and very low amplitude, with periods of the order of five to six minutes. The trigger is whatever produces that displacement: a submarine earthquake, overwhelmingly a thrust event on a subduction interface, most destructively one shallower than fifty kilometres and above magnitude 7.5, rupturing from a few kilometres to more than a thousand; a submarine landslide, often itself earthquake-triggered; a volcanic eruption or caldera collapse, as at Krakatoa in 1883; or a bolide impact. Horizontal sea-floor motion usually generates nothing, so not every undersea earthquake is tsunamigenic. Sources cluster in and around oceanic trenches, chiefly in the Pacific, with the Sunda Trench the Indian Ocean's principal source.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 3 for vertical displacement of the whole water column identified as the mechanism, distinct from the trigger; 2 for the explicit statement that horizontal motion usually generates none; 2 for the depth, magnitude and rupture-extent conditions; 2 for all four triggers with one named example; 1 for trench geography as the answer to "where". Deduct 2 for "tidal wave" and 2 for presenting a submarine earthquake as the mechanism rather than the trigger.

## Lesson 12 — Propagation, shoaling and coastal consequence

Progress: 12 / 16  |  Stage: Core  |  Subtopic: Shallow-water wave theory derived from first principles, the amplification that follows, and the full consequence set of a basin-wide event

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner, tsunami block (deep-water behaviour, shoaling, drawback and the warning caution); Advanced owner §"2004 Indian Ocean Tsunami & India's Warning System" in full, including the correction that **a single elevation cut-off is not a universal safety rule**; D.R. Khullar, printed pp.229-237 (speed as the square root of depth times gravity; the quantified shoaling table of Fig. 8.7; the propagation diagram Fig. 8.4; the 1896 Sanriku case; Figs. 8.5 and 8.6 on the 2004 tectonics and the India-Sri Lanka effects; Tables 8.2 and 8.3; the Indian warning system); Majid Husain, printed pp.4.13-4.14 (speeds and near-source heights).
CA search: "INCOIS Indian Tsunami Early Warning Centre mandate detection network" — run for this lesson, fetched from the Centre's own site.
CA found: 📰 **Verified, first-party.** The **Indian Tsunami Early Warning Centre (ITEWC)** is recorded as established at **INCOIS, Hyderabad**, **under the Ministry of Earth Sciences**, *"in response to December 26, 2004 tsunami"*, and **operational since October 2007**. The system *"comprises a real-time seismic monitoring and sea-level (Tsunami buoys and Tide gauges) network"*, supplemented by **numerical modelling** to assess tsunami potential at coastal locations, and is *"operational round the clock on all days"*. It is *"capable of detecting tsunamigenic earthquakes occurring in the Indian Ocean as well as in the Global Oceans **within 10 minutes** of the occurrence of the earthquake"*, disseminating advisories by **email, fax, SMS, GTS and website**, and *"providing advisories to all Indian Ocean rim countries as part of UNESCO-IOC framework"*.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

```text
 (A) THE DERIVATION — you can COMPUTE the amplification, not just assert it

   A tsunami's wavelength is HUNDREDS of km; ocean depth is a few km.
   Wavelength >> depth  ==> it is a SHALLOW-WATER WAVE EVERYWHERE,
   even in the deep ocean. For such a wave:

            c = sqrt( g x d )      g = 9.8 m/s^2 ;  d = water depth

   WORKED, against the source's own tabulated values:

     d = 4,000 m   c = sqrt(9.8 x 4000) = sqrt(39,200) = 198.0 m/s
                     = 198.0 x 3.6 = 712.8 km/h        [source: 713 km/h]
     d =    50 m   c = sqrt(9.8 x 50)   = sqrt(490)    =  22.1 m/s
                     = 79.7 km/h                        [source:  79 km/h]
     d =    10 m   c = sqrt(9.8 x 10)   = sqrt(98)     =   9.9 m/s
                     = 35.6 km/h                        [source:  36 km/h]

   AND THE WAVELENGTH FOLLOWS, because PERIOD IS CONSERVED:
            L = c x T           T is set at the SOURCE and does not change

     4,000 m : L = 213 km  ->  T = 213/712.8 h = 0.299 h = ~17.9 min
        50 m : L =  23 km  ->  T =  23/79.7  h = 0.289 h = ~17.3 min
        10 m : L = 10.6 km ->  T = 10.6/35.6 h = 0.298 h = ~17.9 min
                                       ^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^
     ==> the SAME period throughout: the source's tabulated wavelengths
         are internally consistent with c = sqrt(gd) and a ~18-minute wave.

 (B) THE PROPAGATION PROFILE — what the numbers mean physically

  DEEP OCEAN (4,000 m)                 SHELF (50 m)        SHORE (10 m)
  speed ~713 km/h                      ~79 km/h            ~36 km/h
  wavelength ~213 km                   ~23 km              ~10.6 km
  amplitude RARELY OVER 1 m            rising               >10 m, occasionally 30 m
  ~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~^^^^^^^^^^^
   - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - - -/^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^
  =================================____________/==================
      deep sea floor              continental slope      shelf   shore
          |                            |                     |
          v                            v                     v
   invisible under a ship      speed FALLS, waves       ENERGY FLUX is
   energy spread through       BUNCH UP (L shrinks)     CONSERVED, so as
   the whole column            front STEEPENS           c and L fall,
                                                        HEIGHT MUST RISE
  THE WAVE TRAIN: 1 -> 2 -> 3 -> 4 -> 5 -> 6 ... the FIRST is not the largest
```

The derivation is the asset. A candidate who can compute 713 km/h at four thousand metres and 36 km/h at ten has demonstrated the physics; one who quotes the numbers has demonstrated a memory.

### 1. Why a tsunami is a shallow-water wave in the deep ocean

⚠️ The term is counter-intuitive and the explanation is one sentence. A wave is "shallow-water" when its **wavelength greatly exceeds the water depth**, so that the whole column participates in its motion. ⚠️ ✅ A tsunami's wavelength is recorded as **often 500 to 700 km**, while the deepest ocean is a few kilometres; the ratio is of the order of a hundred to one. ❗ **A tsunami is therefore a shallow-water wave everywhere in the ocean**, which is exactly why the simple relation **c = √(gd)** applies across its whole journey and why the derivation above works at four thousand metres as well as at ten.

✅ The consequences, as recorded: speed is **500 to 800 km/h** in deep water; ✅ Majid's complementary figures are **100 to 150 km/h** rising to **650 to 950 km/h**; and ✅ deep-water amplitude **rarely exceeds 1 m**. ⚠️ The spread between those speed ranges is itself instructive — speed depends on depth, so a single quoted "tsunami speed" is meaningless without the depth attached.

### 2. Shoaling — why the wave grows, stated as a conservation argument

⚠️ The energy flux carried by the wave train is approximately conserved as it travels. As it enters shallower water:

- **Speed falls**, because c = √(gd) and d is falling.
- **Wavelength falls**, because the period is set at the source and does not change, so L = cT shrinks with c.
- ❗ **Height must therefore rise**, because the same energy flux is being carried by a slower, shorter wave: the energy is compressed into a smaller horizontal extent and has nowhere to go but up.

✅ The outcome as recorded: on shoaling, **height exceeds 10 m and occasionally 30 m**; ✅ Majid adds that **in the vicinity of the epicentre the waves can reach over 30 metres (100 feet)**. ⚠️ Run-up — the vertical height above sea level reached by the water on land — can exceed the offshore wave height again, depending on coastal form.

❗ **The case that makes shoaling unforgettable.** ✅ The **Great Sanriku tsunami of 15 June 1896**, on the Honshu coast, had a deep-water height of only **about 40 centimetres** — and it **ravaged 275 km of coast and killed 28,000 people**. ⚠️ **Forty centimetres in the open ocean; twenty-eight thousand dead on the shore.** That single pairing kills the "small wave, small tsunami" misconception more effectively than any derivation, and it should be written into any answer that has room for one example.

⚠️ **Coastal form modulates the outcome.** A gently shelving, funnel-shaped or embayed coast concentrates and amplifies; a steep, deep-water coast reflects more and amplifies less. ❗ This is why **run-up varies enormously along a single coastline in a single event**, and it is the physical reason behind the correction in §5.

### 3. The 2004 event: the India-specific consequence set

⚠️ This is the second half of the 2025 demand, and the Indian material available to this edition is unusually rich, quantified and multi-dimensional. ✅ All figures below are **as recorded in the named source**, with their limitations stated.

**Human and economic.** ✅ Table 8.2 records deaths as **Indonesia 1,13,306 · Sri Lanka 30,196 · India 15,160 · Thailand 5,186 · others 20,842**, with **11 countries affected**. ✅ Table 8.3 records the Indian financial loss in ₹ crore as **Tamil Nadu 2,730.70 · Andaman and Nicobar 2,500.00 · Kerala 1,358.62 · Andhra Pradesh 720.73 · Puducherry 512.00**.

**Physiographic — the dimension that makes this a geography answer.** ✅ **Trinket Island was split in two**; ✅ **Car Nicobar subsided**; ✅ **beaches vanished at Campbell Bay**; and ✅ **Indira Point, India's southernmost tip, was almost completely washed out**. ⚠️ A tsunami is therefore not only a destructive event but a **geomorphic agent**: it rearranges coastlines, changes island elevations and erases depositional landforms in hours.

**Agricultural and pedological.** ✅ Soil pH after inundation ranged from **6.8 to 9.10**, with **9,500 hectares rendered unfit for cultivation in Nagapattinam**, a loss of about **₹5.2 crore**; reclamation proceeded by **Cauvery flushing plus about 2 tonnes of gypsum per hectare over three to four years**. ⚠️ Salinisation is the slowest-healing of all tsunami consequences, which is why the Core owner lists **salinisation of soil and groundwater** among the standard consequence set.

**Ecological.** ✅ **45 per cent of fragile coral reefs were destroyed**, with regrowth estimated at **700 to 800 years**. ✅ The Andaman reefs are of the **fringing** type and ✅ **mangroves act as nursery habitat**; ✅ India's marine exports, of the order of **US$1.3 billion**, were hit, and aquaculture was affected **from Kerala to Odisha**. ❗ 🔁 Reef ecology beyond this damage statistic belongs to the environment owner.

**Infrastructural and nuclear-safety.** ✅ **Sea water entered the Kalpakkam nuclear plant**. ✅ In Sri Lanka a **rail derailment killed about 1,000 people**. ✅ About **half of Car Nicobar's roughly 20,000 people** were reported missing, and ✅ **Car Nicobar, Katchal, Nancowry, Campbell Bay, Champion, Chowra and Teresa** are named among the affected islands.

**Planetary.** ⚠️ The source records effects on the Earth's rotation and axis attributed to named researchers at the **United States Geological Survey** and **NASA**. ❗ **No figure for those effects is reproduced here**, because the values were revised and this edition holds no first-party statement of them.

❗ **Magnitude reporting, handled under Lesson 10's discipline.** ✅ The source reports the event's magnitude as **8.9, subsequently upgraded to 9.3 by Northwestern University**. ❗ **This edition therefore presents the magnitude as a reported and revised estimate with its source attached, and does not assert a settled figure** — which is exactly the behaviour Lesson 10 argued for, demonstrated on the most-studied earthquake in the region's history.

### 4. The Indian warning system — detection capability, and where this topic stops

✅ Khullar records the pre-2004 position and the Indian response: **no Indian Ocean warning system existed before 26 December 2004**; the Sumatra tsunami took **about three hours** to reach the Indian coast; India built its **own** system at **₹125 crore** rather than joining the Pacific system; it was **inaugurated on 15 October 2007**; and it is a **real-time network of seismic stations, bottom pressure recorders (BPR) and tide gauges**.

📰 ✅ The institutional docket **independently corroborates and completes that account**. The **Indian Tsunami Early Warning Centre (ITEWC)** was established at **INCOIS, Hyderabad**, **under the Ministry of Earth Sciences**, *"in response to December 26, 2004 tsunami"*, and has been **operational since October 2007** — which confirms Khullar's inauguration date from a first-party source. The system *"comprises a real-time seismic monitoring and sea-level (Tsunami buoys and Tide gauges) network"*, uses **numerical modelling** to assess tsunami potential at particular coastal locations, runs **round the clock**, and is *"capable of detecting tsunamigenic earthquakes occurring in the Indian Ocean as well as in the Global Oceans **within 10 minutes** of the occurrence of the earthquake"*. It disseminates advisories by **email, fax, SMS, GTS and website**, and *"is providing advisories to all Indian Ocean rim countries as part of UNESCO-IOC framework."*

⚠️ **Why the ten-minute figure is a geography fact and not an administrative one.** Set it against §2's arithmetic. A tsunami crossing water four kilometres deep travels at about **713 km/h**. Ten minutes of detection latency therefore costs roughly **120 km** of the wave's journey — trivial for a source off Sumatra three hours away, and close to decisive for a **near-field** source in the Andaman segment identified at Lesson 11. ❗ **Warning capability must be read against travel time, and travel time is a function of bathymetry.** That is the sentence that converts an institutional fact into a geographical argument.

❗ **Where Topic 03 stops, stated precisely.** 🔁 **The advisory-to-evacuation chain, community preparedness programmes, the UNESCO-IOC Tsunami Ready indicator framework, the Sendai Framework linkage, the national implementing board and the state-level pilot villages all belong to the disaster-management owner** and are **named once here and not taught**. ⚠️ What Topic 03 owns is the **physical system**: detection latency, the sensor types and why each is needed — seismic stations to detect the earthquake, **bottom pressure recorders** to confirm that a wave has actually been generated in the deep ocean, and **tide gauges** to measure it as it arrives.

### 5. The eight-metre rule — a correction, stated as a correction

❗ ✅ Khullar lists four natural tsunami warnings, the fourth being that coastal areas **less than eight metres above sea level** should be evacuated immediately.

⚠️ **The Advanced owner corrects this explicitly, and the correction is taught here:** *"a single elevation cut-off is not a universal safety rule because run-up varies with bathymetry and coastal form."* ❗ **Khullar's eight-metre rule is therefore cited only as a dated simplification**, not as guidance.

⚠️ **Why a single number cannot work, and the reason is §2's physics.** Run-up depends on offshore bathymetry, the shape of the coast, the wave period and the direction of approach; the same event produces run-ups differing by an order of magnitude along one coastline. ❗ An elevation threshold that is conservative on a steep coast is lethally inadequate in a funnel-shaped bay. ⚠️ The Advanced owner's replacement guidance is correct and transferable: the natural warnings worth teaching are **strong or long coastal shaking, an unusual roar, or rapid sea-level change**, and the instruction is to **evacuate to officially identified high ground or inland routes**, not to a remembered contour.

❗ **And a second correction on the same point.** ⚠️ **Drawback — the withdrawal of the sea before a wave arrives — does not precede every wave.** It occurs when the trough of the train arrives first, which depends on the geometry of the source relative to the coast. ❗ Teaching drawback as a universal warning sign is dangerous, and the Core owner says so: people *"must respond to official warnings and strong/long coastal shaking"*, not to the absence of a drawback.

### 6. 🇮🇳 India hook — Indira Point, Nagapattinam, and a coastline rewritten in hours

🇮🇳 India's single most compact demonstration that a tsunami is a geomorphic agent is a set of four changes recorded on one page: ✅ **Trinket Island split in two**, ✅ **Car Nicobar subsided**, ✅ **beaches vanished at Campbell Bay**, and ✅ **Indira Point — India's southernmost tip — almost completely washed out**. ⚠️ A location that defines the country's southern extremity was substantially destroyed by a wave generated roughly a thousand kilometres away. 🔁 The extent and location convention that makes Indira Point significant belongs to the planetary-and-location topic; Topic 03 takes it purely as **consequence**.

🇮🇳 And the mainland counterpart is agricultural: ✅ **9,500 hectares rendered unfit for cultivation in Nagapattinam**, with soil pH between **6.8 and 9.10**, reclaimed only over **three to four years** by **Cauvery flushing and about two tonnes of gypsum per hectare**. ⚠️ **The wave lasted minutes; the salinity lasted years.** ❗ That asymmetry between event duration and consequence duration is the most useful single idea in a consequences answer, and it transfers directly to the reef statistic — **45 per cent destroyed, 700 to 800 years to re-form**.

❗ **Limits.** ⚠️ All figures in this section are **as recorded in a named source**, several are **book-era compilations**, and casualty and loss figures for the 2004 event were revised repeatedly. ❗ **This edition reports them with attribution and does not present any of them as a settled current figure.**

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| | Deep ocean | Continental shelf | Shoreline |
|---|---|---|---|
| ✅ Depth | ~4,000 m | ~50 m | ~10 m |
| ⚠️ Speed, derived from c = √(gd) | ~**713 km/h** | ~**79 km/h** | ~**36 km/h** |
| ✅ Wavelength | ~**213 km** | ~**23 km** | ~**10.6 km** |
| ✅ Amplitude | **Rarely over 1 m** | Rising | **> 10 m, occasionally 30 m** |
| Detectability | ✅ **Passes unnoticed beneath a ship**; needs bottom pressure recorders | Tide gauges begin to register | Visible and catastrophic |
| Energy | Spread through the whole column over a long wavelength | Compressing | Concentrated into height and run-up |

**Strongest objection.** The derivation is physics, not geography. A geography answer should describe the coast, not compute wave speeds.

**Reply.** The derivation **is** the geography, because it shows that the hazard at any point on a coast is a function of **bathymetry** — which is a map variable. ⚠️ Speed, wavelength and therefore height are all set by water depth; a wide, shallow shelf slows and amplifies the wave over a long approach, a steep margin does less. ❗ **This is why two coasts facing the same source experience different events**, why run-up maps are not concentric, and why microzonation of coastal risk is possible at all. ⚠️ A candidate who can write *"speed varies as the square root of depth, so a wide shallow shelf both slows and amplifies the wave"* has converted a formula into a coastal-geography statement in one clause.

⚠️ **Residual.** The conservation argument in §2 is an idealisation: real tsunamis lose energy to bottom friction, breaking and reflection, and gain locally through refraction and resonance in bays. ❗ The derived figures are therefore **sound for the open-ocean propagation and the order of magnitude at the shore**, and they are **not** a run-up prediction for any particular site.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** "important Geophysical phenomena such as... Tsunami" (Mains GS-I, direct ownership); the GS-III disaster clause in a **support** capacity only, since warning governance is routed.
- **Verified linkage:** **carries the second half of the 2025 General Studies Paper I demand — consequences, 10 marks, about 150 words** — and, with Lesson 11, completes the owned answer. ⚠️ The ledger routes the question to the disaster-management owner while **reserving physical-process ownership to Geography**, which is exactly the division this lesson observes.
- **Probable framing:** consequences should be written in **dimensions, not as a list**: human, physiographic, agricultural, ecological, infrastructural. One named Indian instance per dimension is worth more than six casualty figures.
- **Traps:** quoting a single "tsunami speed" without a depth; saying the wave "grows" without the conservation argument; treating the eight-metre rule as guidance; teaching drawback as a universal warning; presenting a casualty or magnitude figure as settled; writing evacuation and relief doctrine into a geography answer.
- **Answer use:** *"Because speed varies as the square root of depth, a tsunami crossing four kilometres of ocean at over seven hundred kilometres an hour slows to about thirty-six at the ten-metre contour; the same energy flux carried by a slower, shorter wave must go into height, which is why a forty-centimetre swell offshore became a twenty-eight-thousand-fatality event on the Sanriku coast in 1896."*

Revision notes:

- ⚠️ **A tsunami is a SHALLOW-WATER wave everywhere**, because ✅ its wavelength (**often 500-700 km**) vastly exceeds ocean depth. Hence **c = √(gd)** throughout.
- **Derived and checked against the source's own table:** **4,000 m → 712.8 km/h** [source 713] · **50 m → 79.7 km/h** [source 79] · **10 m → 35.6 km/h** [source 36]. **Wavelengths:** ✅ **213 km at 4,000 m · 23 km at 50 m · 10.6 km at 10 m** — all consistent with a conserved period of about **18 minutes**, since **L = cT**.
- ✅ Deep-water speed **500-800 km/h**; ✅ Majid: **100-150 km/h** rising to **650-950 km/h**. ❗ **Speed is meaningless without a depth.** ✅ Deep-water amplitude **rarely exceeds 1 m**; on shoaling **height exceeds 10 m, occasionally 30 m**; ✅ **over 30 m (100 ft) near the epicentre**.
- **Shoaling as conservation:** energy flux ≈ conserved; **c falls** (d falls) → **L falls** (period fixed) → ❗ **height must rise**.
- ✅ ❗ **Great Sanriku, Honshu, 15 June 1896** — deep-water height **~40 cm**, yet **275 km of coast ravaged** and **28,000 killed**. The decisive anti-misconception case. ⚠️ **The first wave is often not the largest**; a **train** of waves minutes to tens of minutes apart.
- ✅ **2004 deaths:** Indonesia **1,13,306** · Sri Lanka **30,196** · **India 15,160** · Thailand **5,186** · others **20,842**; **11 countries**. ✅ **Indian loss (₹ cr):** TN **2,730.70** · A&N **2,500.00** · Kerala **1,358.62** · AP **720.73** · Puducherry **512.00**.
- 🇮🇳 ✅ **Physiographic change:** **Trinket Island split in two** · **Car Nicobar subsided** · **beaches vanished at Campbell Bay** · ❗ **Indira Point, India's southernmost tip, almost completely washed out**.
