- 🇮🇳 ✅ **Nagapattinam: 9,500 ha unfit**, soil pH **6.8-9.10**, loss ~**₹5.2 crore**, reclaimed by **Cauvery flushing + ~2 t/ha gypsum over 3-4 years**. ⚠️ **The wave lasted minutes; the salinity lasted years.**
- ✅ **45 % of fragile coral reefs destroyed; 700-800 years to re-form.** ✅ A&N reefs are **fringing**; ✅ mangroves are **nursery habitat**; ✅ ~**US$1.3 bn** marine exports hit; aquaculture affected **Kerala to Odisha**. ✅ **Sea water entered the Kalpakkam nuclear plant.** ✅ Sri Lanka **rail derailment ~1,000 dead**. ✅ ~half of **Car Nicobar's ~20,000** reported missing; ✅ **Katchal, Nancowry, Campbell Bay, Champion, Chowra, Teresa** named.
- ❗ **Magnitude discipline:** reported as **8.9, upgraded to 9.3 by Northwestern University** — a **reported and revised estimate**, not a settled figure.
- ✅ **Warning system:** **no Indian Ocean system before 26 Dec 2004**; the wave took **about three hours** to reach India; India built its **own** at **₹125 crore** rather than joining the **Pacific** system; **inaugurated 15 October 2007**; **seismic stations + bottom pressure recorders + tide gauges**.
- 📰 ✅ **ITEWC at INCOIS, Hyderabad, under the Ministry of Earth Sciences**, *"in response to December 26, 2004 tsunami"*, **operational since October 2007**; **real-time seismic + sea-level (tsunami buoys and tide gauges) network** plus **numerical modelling**; **round the clock**; ❗ **detects tsunamigenic earthquakes in the Indian Ocean and global oceans within 10 minutes**; advisories by **email, fax, SMS, GTS and website**; **advisories to all Indian Ocean rim countries under the UNESCO-IOC framework**.
- ⚠️ **Ten minutes ≈ 120 km of travel at 713 km/h** — trivial for a far-field source, near-decisive for a **near-field** Andaman source. **Warning capability must be read against travel time, and travel time against bathymetry.**
- ❗ **Correction:** ✅ Khullar's **"below eight metres, evacuate"** is a **dated simplification**; ⚠️ *"a single elevation cut-off is not a universal safety rule because run-up varies with bathymetry and coastal form."* Teach **strong or long coastal shaking, an unusual roar, rapid sea-level change**, and **officially identified high ground**. ❗ **Drawback does not precede every wave.** Do not teach it as a universal sign.
- 🔁 **Routed:** advisory-to-evacuation chain, community preparedness, the UNESCO-IOC Tsunami Ready indicator framework, the Sendai linkage, the national board and state pilot villages — **named once, not taught**.

### Concept check

**Question:** A tsunami generated in water 4,000 m deep is recorded by a mid-ocean instrument as a rise of less than one metre. A coastal official concludes that the threat is negligible and stands down the alert. Using the physics of propagation, explain why the conclusion is wrong, and quantify the error.

**Model answer:** The conclusion mistakes **amplitude** for **energy**. A tsunami is a shallow-water wave even in the deep ocean, because its wavelength — of the order of hundreds of kilometres — vastly exceeds the depth, so its speed is the square root of the product of gravitational acceleration and depth. At 4,000 m that is about 198 metres per second, or roughly **713 km/h**, with a wavelength of about 213 km. An amplitude below one metre spread over that wavelength represents an enormous displaced volume, because the motion extends through the **entire water column**. As the wave enters shallower water its speed falls — to about **79 km/h** at the 50-metre contour and about **36 km/h** at ten metres — and because the period is fixed at the source, the wavelength falls in proportion, to roughly 23 km and 10.6 km. The energy flux is approximately conserved, so as the wave slows and shortens its **height must rise**, commonly beyond ten metres and occasionally to thirty. The quantified refutation is historical: the Great Sanriku tsunami of 15 June 1896 had a deep-water height of about **forty centimetres**, and it ravaged 275 km of the Honshu coast and killed 28,000 people.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat deep-ocean wave height as a measure of tsunami severity. It is the least informative quantity in the system; the informative quantities are the displaced volume, the period and the bathymetry of the approach, and height is simply what the energy becomes when the water runs out of depth.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** Explain the consequences of a basin-wide tsunami, distinguishing effects that end with the event from those that outlast it. *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** Immediate consequences end with the inundation: drowning, destruction of settlements, ports and transport, and disrupted power and communication. Three classes outlast the event by orders of magnitude. The first is **physiographic**: in 2004 Trinket Island was split in two, Car Nicobar subsided, beaches vanished at Campbell Bay and Indira Point, India's southernmost tip, was almost completely washed out — permanent changes to a coastline made in hours. The second is **pedological**: saline inundation raised soil pH to between 6.8 and 9.10 and rendered 9,500 hectares unfit for cultivation at Nagapattinam, requiring flushing and about two tonnes of gypsum per hectare over three to four years. The third is **ecological**: 45 per cent of fragile coral reefs were destroyed, with regrowth estimated at 700 to 800 years, while mangrove nursery habitat and marine exports were damaged together. The qualification is that all such figures are reported estimates, repeatedly revised.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 3 for the event-duration against consequence-duration distinction used as the organising principle; 3 for at least three named Indian physiographic changes; 2 for the salinisation case with its reclamation timescale; 1 for the reef statistic with its regrowth estimate; 1 for the estimates-are-revised qualification. Deduct 2 for a casualty-list answer with no dimensional structure and 2 for writing evacuation or relief doctrine.

## Lesson 13 — Ground behaviour: site effects, amplification, liquefaction and secondary hazards

Progress: 13 / 16  |  Stage: Applied  |  Subtopic: Why identical shaking produces unequal damage, the physics of liquefaction, and the geomorphological map that predicts it

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §9 in full (site amplification, liquefaction, lateral spreading, resonance, slope failure and rockfall, surface rupture; the geomorphology argument; the damage identity; the factual caution against quoting depth, magnitude or casualty figures from memory); Advanced owner §1 and §5 (liquefaction as an India-applied hazard; the trap that alluvial plains are safest); D.R. Khullar, printed pp.221-226 (the 1934 slump belt; the 2001 Kachchh liquefaction; Bhuj's topographical changes; the Muzaffarabad-Hokkaido contrast; building-shape guidance; the Varunavat Parvat sequence).
CA search: "seismic hazard microzonation definition National Center for Seismology GIS site-specific" — run for this lesson, fetched from the Centre's own page.
CA found: 📰 **Verified, first-party.** The National Center for Seismology defines **seismic hazard microzonation** as *"a process of classifying a region into zones of relatively similar exposure to various earthquake-related effects"*, which *"has emerged as a major tool towards providing user-friendly, GIS-based and site-specific hazard and risk related information products to enable appropriate planning of pre- and post-disaster management strategies"*, with the mandate held by the **SHRA unit** of the Centre. The **programme and city list** are a separate, later item and are carried at Lesson 15, not duplicated here.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

```text
 (A) THE SITE-RESPONSE STACK — one earthquake, three sites, three outcomes

   SITE A: HARD ROCK      SITE B: FIRM SOIL      SITE C: SOFT SATURATED
                                                  ALLUVIUM / FILL
   surface  |  ^          surface  |  ^^          surface  |  ^^^^^^
            |  |                   |  ||                   |  ||||||
   ~~~~~~~~~+~~~~~        ~~~~~~~~~+~~~~~~        ~~~~~~~~~+~~~~~~~~~
   | rock      |          | 10-30 m soil|         |  50-200 m soft  |
   | Vs HIGH   |          | Vs MEDIUM   |         |  sediment,      |
   |           |          |=============|         |  Vs LOW, WATER  |
   |===========|          | rock        |         |  TABLE HIGH     |
   | incoming  |          | incoming    |         |=================|
   | wave      |          | wave        |         | rock, incoming  |
        ^                      ^                        ^
        |                      |                        |
     SAME INPUT MOTION ARRIVES AT THE BASE OF ALL THREE COLUMNS
        |                      |                        |
   amplitude ~x1          amplitude UP          amplitude UP STRONGLY,
   short duration         duration UP           duration UP, long periods
                                                 FAVOURED
   WHY: as a wave passes from stiff rock into soft sediment its velocity
   FALLS; to carry the same energy flux the AMPLITUDE must RISE. The soft
   column also TRAPS and REVERBERATES the wave -> longer shaking.

 (B) LIQUEFACTION MICRO-DIAGRAM — the grains let go

   BEFORE                         DURING STRONG SHAKING      AFTER
   o o o o o   grains in          o  o   o  o                o o o o
    o o o o    CONTACT, load       o  o o   o   grains        o o o o  grains
   o o o o o   carried by the     o   o  o  o   SUSPENDED,   o o o o   RESETTLE,
    o o o o    grain skeleton      o o   o  o   load carried  o o o o  DENSER
   ~~water~~   pore water is      ~~~~~~~~~~~~  by WATER     ~~~~~~~~  VOLUME LOST
               PASSIVE            pore pressure RISES                  -> SETTLEMENT
                                  effective stress -> 0
   REQUIREMENT: LOOSE + WATER-SATURATED + UNIFORMLY GRADED SAND
   RESULT: buildings SINK or TILT; buried tanks and pipes FLOAT UP;
           on a slope or near a free face the mass SPREADS SIDEWAYS
```

The stack is why the damage map is not a circle; the micro-diagram is why a building can fail while its own structure is undamaged. Both are statements about **the ground**, which is this lesson's entire subject.

### 1. The question this lesson answers

⚠️ After an earthquake the commonest observation is that damage was **patchy**: one quarter of a city flattened, an adjacent quarter intact, with no difference in magnitude, distance or depth between them. ❗ **The explanation is almost never the earthquake. It is the ground and the buildings.** The Core owner promotes this material into the general-process tier precisely because it explains *"the single most examinable fact about earthquake damage — that the ground, not only the earthquake, determines the loss."*

### 2. The six ground effects, with their mechanisms

| ⚠️ Effect | ⚠️ Mechanism | ⚠️ Consequence |
|---|---|---|
| **Site amplification** | Seismic waves **slow down and grow in amplitude** when they pass from hard rock into soft, unconsolidated sediment | **Alluvial basins, reclaimed land and lake beds shake far harder** than adjacent rock sites in the same earthquake |
| **Liquefaction** | Strong shaking of **loose, water-saturated, uniformly graded sand** raises pore-water pressure until grains lose contact and the deposit temporarily behaves as a fluid | Buildings **sink, tilt or float**; buried tanks and pipes **rise**; foundations fail **even where the structure itself is undamaged** |
| **Lateral spreading** | Liquefied ground on a gentle slope or near a **free face** moves sideways | Roads, embankments, quay walls and pipelines **pulled apart** |
| **Resonance** | A building's **natural period matches** the dominant period of shaking at that site | **Certain building heights fail selectively** while their neighbours survive |
| **Slope failure and rockfall** | Shaking triggers mass movement on already marginal slopes | **The dominant damage mode in mountainous seismic belts** |
| **Surface rupture** | Fault displacement reaches the ground surface | Anything built **across the fault trace is severed** — ❗ a **siting** problem, not a design problem |

❗ **The last row carries a policy distinction worth stating.** ⚠️ Amplification, liquefaction and resonance can all be engineered against — by ground improvement, deeper foundations or changed structural period. **Surface rupture cannot.** A building astride an active fault trace will be torn apart however it is built, so the only remedy is **not to build there**, which makes it a land-use decision rather than a code decision.

⚠️ **Resonance is the effect that produces the most counter-intuitive damage pattern.** Soft, deep sediment favours **long-period** shaking; tall buildings have **long natural periods**. The result is that in a basin, **mid-rise or high-rise structures of a particular height range can fail selectively** while low-rise buildings beside them survive — the opposite of the intuition that taller means more vulnerable in general.

### 3. Liquefaction is a geomorphological prediction, not only an engineering one

⚠️ This is the Core owner's most transferable argument and it is the reason liquefaction belongs in a geography paper at all. **The susceptible material — young, loose, saturated, uniformly graded sand — is a depositional product.** It occurs in **floodplains, deltas, coastal plains, reclaimed land and old channel fills**. ❗ **Liquefaction risk therefore maps onto geomorphology**, which means it can be **anticipated from a landform map before any drilling is done**.

```text
   FROM LANDFORM TO LIQUEFACTION RISK — a map-reading chain

   LANDFORM ON THE MAP          IMPLIED DEPOSIT              RISK
   ------------------------     ------------------------     --------------
   active floodplain       -->  young loose sand, high   -->  HIGH
   meander scar / old           water table
   channel fill            -->  uniformly graded sand    -->  HIGH
   delta / estuarine flat  -->  saturated, very young    -->  HIGH
   reclaimed land / fill   -->  uncompacted, saturated   -->  HIGH
   older terrace (higher,
   dissected, oxidised)    -->  denser, lower water      -->  MODERATE/LOW
                                table
   rock outcrop / plateau  -->  no susceptible deposit   -->  LOW
   ==> THE GEOMORPHOLOGICAL MAP IS A FIRST-ORDER HAZARD MAP.
       Drilling then refines it; it does not replace it.
```

📰 ⚠️ **This is also precisely what the institutional definition of microzonation describes.** The National Center for Seismology defines seismic hazard microzonation as *"a process of classifying a region into zones of relatively similar exposure to various earthquake-related effects"*, delivering *"user-friendly, GIS-based and site-specific hazard and risk related information products."* ❗ **"Zones of relatively similar exposure to earthquake-related effects" is a restatement of the chain above**: the zones are drawn on ground conditions, and ground conditions are, at first order, landform units. 🔁 The programme, its completed and ongoing cities and its governance significance belong to Lesson 15.

### 4. The Indian evidence: a 320-kilometre slump belt and a rift-basin liquefaction field

✅ **The 1934 Bihar-Nepal earthquake** produced the single most striking Indian liquefaction record available to this edition: a **slump belt running from Bettiah to Purnea, of the order of 320 km**, in which **buildings tilted or sank in soft alluvium**. ⚠️ Three hundred and twenty kilometres is not a site effect; it is a **regional** one, and it is the strongest possible refutation of the idea that liquefaction is a local engineering curiosity.

✅ **The 2001 Kachchh earthquake** produced **large-scale liquefaction in the Rann of Kachchh** — a setting of saturated, low-lying, young sediment in a seismically active rift basin, which is the §3 chain satisfied on every term.

✅ **The same event, as a lesson in secondary effects.** The **Bhuj earthquake of 26 January 2001** destroyed **Bhuj, Bhachau, Anjar, Gandhidham and Ratnal**, with damage recorded at about **₹2,000 crore**. ✅ Its **topographical changes** are the geographer's material: **offsets, fissures, scarps, coastal uplift and subsidence**. ⚠️ An earthquake that rearranges a coastline's elevation is doing geomorphic work of the same kind as the tsunami in Lesson 12, over a different footprint.

✅ **And the delayed-failure sequence worth memorising**, because it shows that seismic damage has a tail: cracks opened on **Varunavat Parvat** by the **1991 Uttarkashi earthquake** culminated in the **2003 Uttarkashi landslide** — twelve years between the trigger and the failure. ✅ Khullar's mass-movement vocabulary for such ground failure is **earth avalanche, earthflow and slump**. ❗ 🔁 The mass-movement taxonomy itself and slope-process analysis belong to the weathering-and-mass-movement owner; **the trigger is Topic 03's, the slope process is theirs**, and this file takes the Varunavat case only as evidence of **delayed seismic consequence**.

### 5. The axiom, and the contrast that makes it an argument

✅ Khullar states it as plainly as it can be stated: ***"It is axiomatic that earthquakes do not kill — collapsed buildings do."***

⚠️ **A slogan is not an argument; the paired contrast that follows it is.** ✅ **Muzaffarabad, October 2005, magnitude 7.4 — over one lakh dead.** ✅ **Hokkaido, October 2004, magnitude 7.1 — only 17 injuries**, attributed to **seismically engineered buildings**. ❗ Two events a year apart, of broadly comparable size, with outcomes differing by four orders of magnitude.

⚠️ **The correct reading is careful, and the carefulness is what earns the mark.** The contrast does **not** isolate building design as the sole variable — population density, focal depth, site conditions, terrain, season and time of day all differ between the two. ❗ What it does establish decisively is that **the physical event does not determine the outcome**, because if it did, two events of similar magnitude could not differ by that factor. ⚠️ The Core owner states the identity in compact form: damage is the product of **hazard × site condition × building stock**. ❗ **That is a three-term identity, and Lesson 15 will show that it is not the richest available formulation.**

⚠️ **The building-shape guidance belongs here only in its physical half.** ✅ Khullar advises avoiding **L, T, U, H, + and O** plans. ⚠️ The physical reasoning is that **re-entrant corners concentrate stress and produce torsion when the centres of mass and stiffness do not coincide**, so an irregular plan twists under horizontal shaking rather than translating with it. ❗ 🔁 **The administration of such guidance — code drafting, approval, enforcement, compliance inspection and retrofitting programmes — belongs to the disaster-management owner and is not taught here.** The physics stays; the governance goes.

### 6. 🇮🇳 India hook — the Indo-Gangetic alluvium as an amplification problem

🇮🇳 India's most consequential site-effect problem is not in its highest seismic zone. ✅ The Advanced owner records that **the Indo-Gangetic basin is high-risk because thick alluvium amplifies shaking**, and it lists among its three standing traps: ❌ *"Alluvial plains are safest during quakes"* → ⚠️ **thick alluvium AMPLIFIES shaking, and can liquefy.**

⚠️ **Why this is the hook that matters most.** The Indo-Gangetic plain combines, in one region, **every term in this lesson's risk chain**: a very thick column of young, unconsolidated, frequently saturated sediment; a very high water table over large areas; the greatest population density in the country; a predominantly non-engineered building stock; and proximity to the Himalayan source zone. ❗ **The hazard is not the highest in India, and the risk may nonetheless be.** ⚠️ That decoupling of hazard from risk is the argument Lesson 14 generalises and Lesson 15 converts into governance.

❗ **Limits, strictly observed.** ⚠️ **No sediment thickness, water-table depth, amplification factor or population figure is asserted here.** The structural statement — thick young saturated sediment plus dense population plus non-engineered construction plus a nearby source — is fully sufficient for a Mains answer, and an invented number would be the one element an examiner could falsify.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| | Rock site | Firm soil site | Soft saturated alluvium or fill |
|---|---|---|---|
| Amplitude of shaking | Lowest | Raised | **Highest** |
| Duration of shaking | Shortest | Longer | **Longest** — the column reverberates |
| Dominant period | Short | Intermediate | **Long** — selectively dangerous to tall structures |
| Liquefaction potential | None | Low | **High**, if loose, saturated and uniformly graded |
| Typical landform | Outcrop, plateau, dissected upland | Older terrace | **Floodplain, delta, channel fill, reclaimed land** |
| Engineering remedy | Rarely needed | Conventional | Ground improvement, deep foundations — ❗ or **do not build** |

**Strongest objection.** This is soil mechanics and structural engineering. The disaster-management syllabus owns it, so Geography should route it rather than teach it.

**Reply.** The boundary is real and this lesson observes it — response doctrine, institutional architecture, code administration and retrofitting programmes are all routed. ⚠️ **What cannot be routed is the ground itself.** ❗ **The susceptible material is a depositional product, so its distribution is a geomorphological fact and its prediction is a map-reading exercise**, which is Geography's competence and nobody else's. ⚠️ The engineer asks how to found a structure on a given soil; the geographer asks **which parts of a region have that soil and why**, and answers it from landform units before a borehole is sunk. ❗ That is also exactly what the institutional definition of microzonation describes, which is why the two converge.

⚠️ **Residual.** Site response is **frequency-dependent and non-linear**: very strong shaking can soften soil enough to *reduce* high-frequency amplification even while increasing duration and settlement. ❗ Treat amplification as a strong general tendency, not as a fixed multiplier, and **quote no amplification factor**.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** "important Geophysical phenomena such as earthquakes" (Mains GS-I, direct ownership) and **"Disaster and disaster management"** (Mains GS-III) in a **support** capacity only — this lesson supplies the physical process and the risk framing, not the institutional content.
- **Verified linkage:** no owned demand sits here alone. It supplies the damage-explanation paragraph for the **2025** tsunami answer's consequence half and for any GS-III vulnerability answer, and it is the physical foundation of Lesson 15's microzonation argument.
- **Probable framing:** two to three sentences inside a larger answer — amplification, liquefaction with its geomorphological mapping, and the axiom with its contrast — followed immediately by the routing sentence that keeps the answer in its lane.
- **Traps:** treating alluvial plains as safe; describing liquefaction as the ground "turning to quicksand" without the pore-pressure mechanism; assuming taller is always more vulnerable; quoting an amplification factor; treating surface rupture as a design problem; writing code-enforcement or relief doctrine into a geography answer; attaching a casualty figure to an Indian earthquake from memory.
- **Answer use:** *"Damage is the product of hazard, site condition and building stock. Because the material that liquefies — young, loose, saturated, uniformly graded sand — is a depositional product, liquefaction risk maps onto floodplains, deltas, channel fills and reclaimed land, and can therefore be anticipated from a landform map before any drilling."*

Revision notes:

- ❗ **Damage is patchy because the ground and the buildings differ, not because the earthquake did.**
- **Six ground effects:** **site amplification** · **liquefaction** · **lateral spreading** · **resonance** · **slope failure and rockfall** · **surface rupture**.
- ⚠️ **Amplification mechanism:** wave velocity **falls** on entering soft sediment → **amplitude rises** to carry the same energy flux; the soft column also **traps and reverberates** the wave → **longer duration**.
- ⚠️ **Liquefaction requires loose + water-saturated + uniformly graded sand.** Pore pressure rises → **effective stress approaches zero** → the deposit behaves as a fluid. Buildings **sink or tilt**; **buried tanks and pipes float up**; failure occurs **even where the structure is undamaged**.
- ⚠️ **Lateral spreading** needs a gentle slope or a **free face**. ⚠️ **Resonance** matches building period to site period — **selective failure by height**, and soft deep basins favour **long periods**, i.e. **taller structures**. ❗ **Surface rupture is a SITING problem, not a design problem** — the only remedy is not to build across the trace.
- ⚠️ ❗ **Liquefaction risk maps onto geomorphology:** floodplains, **old channel fills**, deltas, coastal plains, **reclaimed land**. **The landform map is a first-order hazard map**; drilling refines it.
- 📰 ✅ **Microzonation is defined** as *"a process of classifying a region into zones of relatively similar exposure to various earthquake-related effects"*, giving **GIS-based, site-specific** products; the **SHRA unit** holds the mandate.
- ✅ **1934 Bihar-Nepal: slump belt from Bettiah to Purnea, ~320 km**, buildings **tilted or sank in soft alluvium** — a **regional**, not local, effect.
- ✅ **2001: large-scale liquefaction in the Rann of Kachchh.** ✅ **Bhuj, 26 January 2001** destroyed **Bhuj, Bhachau, Anjar, Gandhidham, Ratnal**; damage ~**₹2,000 crore**; topographical change — **offsets, fissures, scarps, coastal uplift and subsidence**.
- ✅ **Varunavat Parvat:** cracks from the **1991 Uttarkashi earthquake** → the **2003 Uttarkashi landslide**. ⚠️ **Seismic damage has a tail.** ✅ Vocabulary: **earth avalanche, earthflow, slump**. 🔁 Slope-process taxonomy routed.
- ✅ ❗ ***"It is axiomatic that earthquakes do not kill — collapsed buildings do."*** ✅ **Muzaffarabad, Oct 2005, M 7.4 — over one lakh dead** against ✅ **Hokkaido, Oct 2004, M 7.1 — only 17 injuries**, attributed to **seismically engineered buildings**.
- ⚠️ The contrast does **not** isolate design as the sole variable; it **does** prove the physical event does not determine the outcome. ⚠️ Owner identity: **damage = hazard × site condition × building stock** (three terms; a richer four-term form arrives at Lesson 15).
- ✅ **Avoid L, T, U, H, + and O plans** — ⚠️ **re-entrant corners concentrate stress; mismatch of mass and stiffness centres causes torsion**. 🔁 Code administration routed.
- 🇮🇳 ✅ **The Indo-Gangetic basin is high-risk because thick alluvium amplifies shaking**; ❌ *"alluvial plains are safest"* is a standing trap. ⚠️ **Hazard is not highest there; risk may nonetheless be** — thick young saturated sediment + density + non-engineered stock + a nearby source. ❗ **No thickness, water-table depth, amplification factor or population figure asserted.**
- ⚠️ **Residual:** site response is **frequency-dependent and non-linear**; ❗ **quote no amplification factor**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Two neighbourhoods of a city lie four kilometres apart and experienced the same earthquake. In the first, built on a dissected rock terrace, damage was light and confined to old masonry. In the second, built on reclaimed land over a filled channel, several six-storey buildings tilted and sank while adjacent two-storey houses were almost untouched, and buried water tanks rose out of the ground. Explain every feature of this pattern.

**Model answer:** Four distinct effects are operating and the pattern is diagnostic of each. **Site amplification** explains the gross contrast: seismic waves entering soft, unconsolidated fill slow down, and must therefore increase in amplitude to carry the same energy flux, while the soft column traps and reverberates them, lengthening the duration. The rock terrace received the same input motion with neither effect. **Liquefaction** explains the sinking and tilting: a filled channel supplies loose, saturated, uniformly graded sand, and strong shaking raises pore-water pressure until the effective stress between grains approaches zero and the deposit temporarily behaves as a fluid. It also explains the **buried tanks rising**, which is a buoyancy effect in liquefied ground and is one of the most reliable field indicators of liquefaction. **Resonance** explains why the six-storey buildings failed selectively while two-storey houses survived: a deep soft column favours long-period shaking, and the natural period of a mid-rise structure matches it far better than that of a low, stiff house. Finally the **light masonry damage on the terrace** shows that the earthquake was genuinely capable of causing loss and that the difference was the ground and the structures, not the event — the pattern is evidence that damage is the product of hazard, site condition and building stock.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not assume that taller buildings are always more vulnerable. Vulnerability depends on the match between structural period and site period, so on soft deep ground mid-rise and high-rise buildings may fail selectively while low-rise ones survive, whereas on stiff ground the relationship can reverse.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "Earthquake risk is a property of the ground as much as of the earthquake." Examine with reference to site effects. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** The claim is well founded. The same input motion produces widely different surface shaking depending on the material it passes through: on entering soft unconsolidated sediment a wave slows, so its amplitude must rise to carry the same energy flux, and the soft column traps and reverberates it, lengthening the duration and favouring long periods. Where that sediment is loose, saturated and uniformly graded, strong shaking raises pore-water pressure until grains lose contact and the deposit liquefies, so buildings sink or tilt and buried structures float up — as along the Bettiah-to-Purnea slump belt of about 320 kilometres in 1934, and across the Rann of Kachchh in 2001. Because such material is a depositional product, risk maps onto floodplains, deltas, channel fills and reclaimed land and can be anticipated from landform units. The qualification is that site response is non-linear and frequency-dependent, so no fixed amplification factor should be asserted.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 3 for the amplification mechanism given as velocity-fall plus energy-flux conservation, with duration included; 3 for liquefaction stated through pore-pressure and effective stress, not as "turning to quicksand"; 2 for the geomorphological mapping argument; 1 for one dated Indian case with its extent; 1 for the non-linearity qualification. Deduct 2 for calling alluvial plains safe and 2 for any quoted amplification factor.

## Lesson 14 — India's seismic setting: plate kinematics, regional gradient and intraplate earthquakes

Progress: 14 / 16  |  Stage: Applied  |  Subtopic: The convergence that drives Indian seismicity, the seven regions and their observed recurrence, and the three events that killed the aseismic-Peninsula assumption

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Khullar, printed pp.219-226 (India's key vulnerabilities; the seven seismic regions; Table 8.1's region-wise counts and return periods; the three broad seismological zones with their analytic labels; Fig. 8.2's seismic-coefficient isolines; the attribution of Koyna, Latur and Jabalpur); Majid Husain, *Indian & World Geography*, printed p.2.21 with Fig. 2.26 (the Himalayan belt with thrust planes; the Indo-Gangetic tract and the Rann of Kachchh; the Peninsula as a more stable block that is not earthquake-free).

The map is what you mark; the matrix is what converts "India is seismic" into a defensible regional gradient. Read the two together and the gradient has a cause on the left and a measurement on the right.

### 1. The driver, stated as kinematics

✅ The Advanced owner states the cause in one sentence: *"The root cause is plate tectonics: the Indian Plate's northerly drift and collision with the Asian Plate built the Himalaya and keeps accumulating strain — released as earthquakes along thrust faults."* ✅ The rate is given as about **5 cm per year** north-north-eastward.

✅ Khullar adds the geodynamic refinement that explains why the belt is still active rather than merely old: the Himalayan zone is **subducting along the Himalayas**, is **not yet in isostatic equilibrium** and is **still rising**. ⚠️ A range still adjusting vertically is a range still accumulating and releasing strain, which is why the Himalayan front is seismically alive in a way that an old, equilibrated orogen is not.

⚠️ **The structural vocabulary of the front**, used once and kept brief: the Himalayan deformation front is organised on a set of north-dipping thrusts — the **Main Frontal Thrust (MFT)** at the surface trace against the plain, the **Main Boundary Thrust (MBT)** behind it and the **Main Central Thrust (MCT)** behind that. ✅ Majid's own description is *"a marked region of instability which is characterised with several thrust planes."* ❗ 🔁 The structural geology and the orogenic chronology of those thrusts belong to the crust-and-rocks topic; Topic 03 takes them only as **the surfaces on which Himalayan earthquakes occur**.

⚠️ **Seismic gaps**, named with their limitation. Segments of the Himalayan front that have not produced a great earthquake in the instrumental or well-documented historical record, while adjacent segments have, are described as **seismic gaps**, and they are regarded as having accumulated unreleased strain. ❗ **The limitation is severe and must be written with the term.** A gap is an **observation about a catalogue**, not a prediction: the catalogue is short relative to the recurrence interval, a gap may reflect aseismic slip rather than locked strain, and ⚠️ **no date, probability or magnitude may be attached to any named gap**. ❗ This edition names the concept and asserts nothing further.

### 2. The three broad seismological zones, with their analytic labels

✅ Khullar's threefold division carries labels that do real analytical work, and each label must be read as **descriptive of observed history**, not as a forecast.

| ✅ Zone | ✅ Label | ✅ Mechanism and content |
|---|---|---|
| **Himalayan** | *"Zone of maximum intensity"* | Indian plate moving north/north-east at **~5 cm/yr**, subducting along the Himalayas, **not yet in isostatic equilibrium and still rising**. ❗ **The stretch between Mount Everest and Badrinath is almost stable**, while the arc through **Mussoorie, Shimla, Kangra, Dalhousie, Gulmarg** and through **Bihar, Assam, eastern Nepal, Sikkim, Bhutan, Arunachal and western Nagaland-Manipur** is **high-magnitude prone** |
| **Indo-Gangetic** | *"Zone of comparative intensity"* | Mostly **magnitude 6 to 6.5**, but ❗ **more harmful because of population density** — and, per Lesson 13, because of alluvial amplification |
| **Peninsular** | *"Zone of minimum intensity"* | ❗ **Disproved as aseismic** by **Koyna 1967**, **Latur 1993** and **Jabalpur 1997** |

❗ **The labels must be normalised and the normalisation stated.** ⚠️ *"Maximum"*, *"comparative"* and *"minimum intensity"* read like forecasts. They are **descriptions of the historical record and of expected design ground motion**, and the entire thrust of Lesson 8 is that **prediction of earthquake timing is not scientifically available**. ❗ **Teach the labels as descriptive; never write that a zone "will" produce an event of any size.**

⚠️ **The Indo-Gangetic row is the most important of the three for a GS answer**, because it is the clearest statement in the Indian literature that **hazard and risk are different quantities**. A region of moderate magnitude can be the most harmful region in the country because of what stands on it.

### 3. The seven seismic regions and the observed recurrence matrix

✅ Khullar divides India into **seven seismic regions**: **(1) Kashmir and the Western Himalayas · (2) Central Himalayas · (3) North-East India · (4) Indo-Gangetic Basin and Rajasthan · (5) Khambhat and the Rann of Kachchh · (6) Peninsular India, including Lakshadweep · (7) Andaman and Nicobar.**

✅ The recurrence matrix reproduced in the visual above is drawn from the same source and covers **magnitude greater than 5.0 events from 1897 to 2014**, with the source's own footnote that the **5.0-5.9 counts are approximate**.

❗ **The normalisation this table requires is the most important in the lesson.** ⚠️ A "return period of less than four months" **does not mean that an earthquake is due every four months**. It means that **over the 117 years of the catalogue, events of that class occurred at an average spacing of less than four months in that region**. ⚠️ Earthquake occurrence is clustered, not periodic: long quiescent intervals and dense swarms both occur, and the mean of a skewed distribution is a poor guide to the next interval. ❗ **Write "observed average recurrence over 1897-2014", never "return period", if there is any risk of the deterministic reading** — and never write that a region is "overdue".

⚠️ **Read correctly, the matrix is extremely valuable**, because it supplies a **ranked gradient with evidence**. North-East India dominates on every count; Andaman and Nicobar is second in frequency at moderate magnitudes; the Central Himalayas carry a high moderate-magnitude count with a smaller great-event count; and **Khambhat and the Rann of Kachchh is the striking entry** — only four recorded events above magnitude 5.0, but **one above 7.0 and one above 8.0**. ❗ **A region can be infrequent and still capable of the largest events in the country**, which is the single best argument against treating frequency as a proxy for hazard.

### 4. Intraplate seismicity, and the end of the aseismic-Peninsula assumption

⚠️ The Peninsular shield is a stable continental region: ✅ Majid calls it *"a more stable block"*, ✅ the Advanced owner calls it *"presumably a stable landmass with few quakes"*, and ✅ the same source's own text adds that the **oldest parts of a plate, the shields, are the least susceptible to earthquakes**. ❗ **All three statements are true and none of them means aseismic.**

✅ The three events that establish this are dated and named: **Koyna 1967**, **Latur 1993** and **Jabalpur 1997**.

✅ **And their attributions differ, which is the analytically interesting part.** Khullar attributes **Koyna to reservoir loading in Shivaji Sagar** — the mechanism of Lesson 8 — while attributing **Latur and Jabalpur to intraplate stress arising from the Indian plate's northward drift**. ⚠️ **Two different mechanisms, one apparently stable block.** The first is a locally triggered release; the second is the far-field transmission of collision stress into the plate interior, which means the collision at the northern margin is **loading the shield itself**.

❗ **The trap, stated in the Advanced owner's own words:** ❌ *"The Peninsular shield is completely aseismic."* → ⚠️ *"It is RELATIVELY stable but not immune — Latur and Koyna are significant intraplate quakes."*

⚠️ **Why intraplate events are disproportionately damaging**, which is the reason this matters beyond trap-avoidance. Stable continental crust is **cold, strong and relatively unfractured**, so seismic waves **attenuate less** and shaking is felt over a wider area than for an equivalent event at a plate margin. ⚠️ Combine that with building stock designed for a low-hazard zone and a population with no recent experience of earthquakes, and a moderate intraplate event can produce losses out of proportion to its magnitude. ❗ **No casualty or magnitude figure is asserted here for Koyna, Latur or Jabalpur.**

⚠️ ✅ **The seismic-coefficient map supports the same gradient from a different direction.** Khullar's epicentre map carries **horizontal seismic coefficient isolines from 0.01 to 0.08**: **low, 0.01-0.02**, over peninsular India, Madhya Pradesh and Rajasthan; **medium, 0.04-0.05**, over the Satluj-Ganga plain, the Godavari basin of Andhra Pradesh, Ratnagiri and Raigad districts and parts of Gujarat; and **high** over Jammu and Kashmir, Himachal Pradesh, Uttarakhand, north Bihar, the north-eastern states, Kachchh and the Andaman and Nicobar Islands. ⚠️ A coefficient map is a **design-parameter map**, which is the bridge to Lesson 15.

### 5. India's multi-hazard profile, with both figures dated

✅ Khullar's compact vulnerability profile situates seismic risk among India's other hazards: **55 per cent of the total area in Seismic Zones III, IV and V**; **68 per cent of the net sown area drought-vulnerable**; **40 million hectares flood-vulnerable**; **8 per cent of the land cyclone-vulnerable**; of **7,516 km of coastline, about 5,700 km prone to cyclones and tsunamis**; the **sub-Himalaya and the Western Ghats landslide-prone**; and the distributional observation that **only West Bengal faces all four disaster types, seven states face three, ten face two and nine face one**.

📰 ✅ **The current official phrasing is different, and the difference must be stated rather than smoothed over.** Government sources give approximately **59 per cent of India's landmass as prone to earthquakes of moderate to severe seismic hazard**, and of India's **7,516 km coastline, close to 5,700 km as vulnerable to cyclones and tsunamis**.

❗ **The two seismic figures are NOT the same statistic and must not be substituted for one another.**

| | ✅ Book-era figure | 📰 Current official figure |
|---|---|---|
| Statement | **55 %** of total area lies in **Seismic Zones III, IV and V** | ~**59 %** of the landmass is prone to earthquakes of **moderate to severe seismic hazard** |
| What it measures | An **area share of three named zones** on a zonation map | An **area share by hazard severity class** |
| Depends on | The zone boundaries of the map in force at the time | The official hazard characterisation in current use |
| Correct handling | Quote **with its era and its zone list** | Quote **as the current official phrasing**, with its source class |

⚠️ **The coastline figure, by contrast, is a genuine cross-validation**: the book-era statement and the current official statement **agree exactly** at about **5,700 km of 7,516 km**. ❗ That agreement is worth noting precisely because it makes the disagreement in the row above meaningful rather than accidental.

❗ 🔁 **Boundary.** Drought, flood, cyclone and landslide shares appear here **only to situate seismic risk in India's hazard profile**. Cyclones belong to the meteorological-hazards topic, floods and landslide processes to the mass-movement and disaster-management owners, and **none of them is developed in this file**.

### 6. 🇮🇳 India hook — the Everest-Badrinath stretch against the Kangra-Dalhousie-Gulmarg arc

🇮🇳 The single most useful fact in Khullar's Himalayan account is a **contrast within the belt itself**. ✅ **The stretch between Mount Everest and Badrinath is almost stable**, while the arc running through **Mussoorie, Shimla, Kangra, Dalhousie and Gulmarg** — and eastward through **Bihar, Assam, eastern Nepal, Sikkim, Bhutan, Arunachal and western Nagaland-Manipur** — is **prone to high-magnitude events**.

⚠️ **Why this is worth more than any zone label.** It establishes that the Himalayan front is **segmented**: the collision is continuous along its length, but the seismic behaviour is not, and that segmentation is a first-order geographical fact about India's most hazardous belt. ❗ An answer that writes *"the Himalaya is Zone V"* has stated a classification; one that writes *"the Himalayan front is segmented, with a comparatively stable stretch between Everest and Badrinath and high-magnitude-prone arcs to its west and east"* has stated a geography.

❗ **Limits, and they matter here more than anywhere else in the lesson.** ⚠️ *"Almost stable"* is the source's own hedge and it is a statement about the **historical record**, which is short. ❗ **It must never be read as "safe", and this edition attaches no probability, no recurrence interval and no magnitude ceiling to the stretch or to the arcs.** ⚠️ Per Lesson 8, a quiet segment may be quiet because it is slipping aseismically or because the catalogue is too short — and those two possibilities have opposite implications.

### Comparison, objection and the residual

| | Himalayan belt | Indo-Gangetic basin | Peninsular shield | Kachchh |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Driver | Plate collision at ~5 cm/yr on thrust planes | Proximity to the collision front; thick alluvium | **Intraplate stress** from the northward drift; local triggers | Intraplate rift reactivation |
| Typical magnitude | Highest in India | ✅ Mostly **6 to 6.5** | Moderate | ✅ Infrequent but includes events **above 7.0 and 8.0** |
| ✅ Observed frequency, 1897-2014 | NE India highest of all; Central Himalayas high | Low | Low | **Lowest count, highest ceiling** |
| Principal damage modifier | Slope failure; mountain terrain | ❗ **Amplification plus population density** | Low attenuation; unprepared stock | Liquefaction in saturated rift sediment |
| ✅ Named events in this file | — | — | **Koyna 1967 · Latur 1993 · Jabalpur 1997** | **Bhuj, 26 January 2001** |

**Strongest objection.** This is the disaster-management syllabus. A Geography paper should stop at the plate boundary.

**Reply.** The division is precise and this lesson respects it. ⚠️ **Topic 03 answers why a region is seismically classified the way it is** — plate kinematics, segmentation, intraplate stress transmission, site conditions and observed recurrence. ❗ 🔁 **Institutional architecture, response doctrine, relief administration and preparedness programmes are routed.** ⚠️ The distinction is visible in the material itself: the **gradient** is a geographical fact derived from tectonics and a catalogue; **what a state does about it** is not.

⚠️ **Residual.** The recurrence matrix rests on a catalogue of about **117 years**, which is **short relative to the recurrence intervals of great earthquakes**, particularly in low-frequency, high-ceiling regions like Kachchh. ❗ **A region with few recorded events may be genuinely quiet or merely under-sampled**, and nothing in the matrix distinguishes the two.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** "important Geophysical phenomena such as earthquakes" (Mains GS-I, direct ownership) and **"Disaster and disaster management"** (Mains GS-III) in a support capacity.
- **Verified linkage:** no owned Mains demand sits here alone; this lesson supplies the **Indian comparative frame** that the 2020 belt answer uses for contrast and that any GS-III vulnerability answer requires. 🔁 The 2021 GS-III demand on India's earthquake vulnerability is **routed to the disaster-management owner**, and this lesson supplies only its physical and distributional half.
- **Probable framing:** an Indian seismicity paragraph should run **driver → gradient → exceptions → modifier**: convergence at about 5 cm/yr; the regional gradient with north-east India at the top; the Peninsular exceptions by name and date; and the amplification-plus-density modifier that makes the Indo-Gangetic plain the risk anomaly.
- **Traps:** calling the Peninsula aseismic; reading return periods as forecasts; saying a region is "overdue"; writing "Zone V" where a mechanism is wanted; substituting the current 59 per cent figure for the book-era 55 per cent figure as though they measured the same thing; attaching a probability to a seismic gap; quoting casualty figures for Koyna, Latur or Jabalpur.
- **Answer use:** *"The gradient follows the kinematics: convergence at about five centimetres a year concentrates the largest events on the Himalayan thrusts and in the north-east, while far-field intraplate stress and local triggering damage the supposedly stable Peninsula — Koyna in 1967, Latur in 1993 and Jabalpur in 1997."*

Revision notes:

- ✅ **Driver:** the Indian plate moves **north/north-east at about 5 cm per year**, colliding with Asia; strain accumulates and is released on **thrust faults**. ✅ The belt is **subducting along the Himalayas, not yet in isostatic equilibrium, and still rising**.
- ⚠️ Front structure: **MFT · MBT · MCT**; ✅ *"a marked region of instability... characterised with several thrust planes."* 🔁 Structural chronology routed.
- ⚠️ **Seismic gaps** are an observation about a **short catalogue**, not a prediction. ❗ **No date, probability or magnitude attached to any gap.**
- ✅ **Three broad zones:** **Himalayan — "zone of maximum intensity"** · **Indo-Gangetic — "zone of comparative intensity"**, ✅ mostly **M 6-6.5** but ❗ **more harmful because of population density** · **Peninsular — "zone of minimum intensity"**, ❗ **disproved as aseismic** by **Koyna 1967, Latur 1993, Jabalpur 1997**. ❗ **Labels are descriptive, not forecasts.**
- ✅ **Seven regions:** Kashmir & W. Himalayas · Central Himalayas · **North-East India** · Indo-Gangetic Basin & Rajasthan · **Khambhat & Rann of Kachchh** · Peninsular India (incl. Lakshadweep) · **Andaman & Nicobar**.
- ✅ **Observed recurrence, 1897-2014:** NE India **200/128/15/4, < 4 months** · A&N **90/80/1/1, < 8 months** · Central Himalayas **68/28/4/1, 1 year** · Kashmir & W. Himalayas **25/8/2/2, 2.5-3 yrs** · Peninsular **31/11/—/—, 2.5-3 yrs** · Indo-Gangetic & Rajasthan **14/6/—/—, 5 yrs** · **Khambhat & Rann of Kachchh 4/4/1/1, 20 yrs**. ✅ Footnote: **5.0-5.9 counts approximate**.
- ❗ **Return period = observed average over 1897-2014, NOT a forecast.** Occurrence is **clustered, not periodic**. ❗ Never write "overdue".
- ❗ **Kachchh: lowest count, highest ceiling** — frequency is **not** a proxy for hazard.
- ⚠️ **Why intraplate events hit hard:** stable continental crust is **cold, strong and less fractured**, so waves **attenuate less** and shaking spreads further; stock is designed for low hazard. ❗ **No casualty or magnitude figures asserted.**
- ✅ **Attributions differ:** **Koyna → reservoir loading in Shivaji Sagar**; **Latur and Jabalpur → intraplate stress from the plate's northward drift**.
- ✅ **Seismic coefficient isolines 0.01-0.08:** **low 0.01-0.02** (peninsular India, MP, Rajasthan) · **medium 0.04-0.05** (Satluj-Ganga plain, Godavari basin of AP, Ratnagiri and Raigad, parts of Gujarat) · **high** (J&K, HP, Uttarakhand, north Bihar, NE states, Kachchh, A&N). ⚠️ A **design-parameter** map.
- ✅ **Book-era profile:** **55 %** of area in **Zones III-IV-V** · **68 %** of net sown area drought-vulnerable · **40 m ha** flood-vulnerable · **8 %** cyclone-vulnerable · **~5,700 km of 7,516 km** coastline prone to cyclones and tsunamis · **only West Bengal faces all four types; seven states three; ten two; nine one**.
- 📰 ✅ **Current official:** ~**59 %** of the landmass prone to **moderate to severe** seismic hazard; **~5,700 km of 7,516 km** coastline vulnerable. ❗ **55 % and 59 % are DIFFERENT statistics** — a three-zone **area share** against a **hazard-severity** share. ⚠️ The coastline figures **agree exactly** — a genuine cross-validation.
- 🇮🇳 ✅ ❗ **The Everest-to-Badrinath stretch is "almost stable"**, while the arc through **Mussoorie, Shimla, Kangra, Dalhousie, Gulmarg** and through **Bihar, Assam, east Nepal, Sikkim, Bhutan, Arunachal, west Nagaland-Manipur** is **high-magnitude prone**. ⚠️ **The front is SEGMENTED.** ❗ *"Almost stable"* ≠ safe; **no probability attached**.
- ⚠️ **Residual:** a **117-year** catalogue is short against great-earthquake recurrence; **quiet may mean under-sampled**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Using the observed recurrence record for 1897-2014, a planner argues: "North-East India records an event above magnitude 5.0 roughly every four months and Kachchh only every twenty years, so investment in seismic safety should be concentrated in the north-east and reduced in Gujarat." Evaluate the argument on three distinct grounds.

**Model answer:** The argument fails on three separate grounds, and each is independently sufficient. **First, it misreads the statistic.** The figures are observed average spacings over a 117-year catalogue, not forecasts; earthquake occurrence is clustered rather than periodic, so a mean drawn from a skewed distribution says little about the next interval, and no region becomes "due" or "safe" by elapsed time. **Second, it confuses frequency with hazard.** The same record shows that Khambhat and the Rann of Kachchh produced only four events above magnitude 5.0 but **one above 7.0 and one above 8.0**, so it is a low-frequency, high-ceiling region. Since energy rises by roughly a factor of a thousand for every two magnitude units, a region's single largest credible event can dominate its expected loss entirely. **Third, it ignores exposure, vulnerability and site condition.** Kachchh combines saturated, low-lying rift sediment, which liquefied on a large scale in 2001, with substantial settlement — the Bhuj earthquake of 26 January 2001 destroyed Bhuj, Bhachau, Anjar, Gandhidham and Ratnal, with damage recorded at about ₹2,000 crore. Loss is the product of hazard, site condition and building stock, so a frequency ranking is not a risk ranking. The defensible conclusion is that both regions require investment, differently targeted.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat a long observed recurrence interval as a measure of safety. A long interval combined with a high magnitude ceiling is the classic profile of a region whose rare events dominate its loss record, and it is the profile in which under-preparedness is most likely because living memory contains no example.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** Account for the regional gradient of seismicity in India, and explain why the most hazardous region is not necessarily the most at risk. *(Account for and explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** The gradient follows the kinematics. The Indian plate moves north-north-east at about five centimetres a year and underthrusts Asia along the Himalayan thrusts, so the largest and most frequent events occur along that collision front, with north-east India the most active region on record and the Central and Western Himalayas next. Away from the front, the Kachchh rift reactivates infrequently but has produced events above magnitude 7.0 and 8.0, while the Peninsular shield, long presumed stable, has produced Koyna in 1967, attributed to reservoir loading, and Latur in 1993 and Jabalpur in 1997, attributed to intraplate stress from the plate's drift. Risk, however, is not hazard. The Indo-Gangetic basin records mostly moderate events of magnitude six to six and a half, yet thick alluvium amplifies shaking and liquefies, and population density and non-engineered construction multiply exposure — so a region of moderate hazard carries the most concentrated risk.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 3 for the convergence rate and thrust mechanism used to derive the gradient rather than assert it; 3 for the hazard-against-risk distinction demonstrated on the Indo-Gangetic case; 2 for the Peninsular exceptions named and dated with their differing attributions; 1 for Kachchh identified as low-frequency and high-ceiling; 1 for the catalogue-length or descriptive-label qualification. Deduct 2 for calling the Peninsula aseismic and 2 for reading a return period as a forecast.

## Lesson 15 — Zonation, microzonation and the design code: what a seismic zone does and does not mean

 (B) DETERMINISTIC AGAINST PROBABILISTIC — the argument underneath the episode

   DETERMINISTIC-LEANING BASIS        |  PROBABILISTIC BASIS (PSHA)
   ---------------------------------  |  ---------------------------------
   "what is the worst credible event  |  "what ground motion has probability
    for this site, and design for it" |   p of being exceeded in T years"

                                      |  model, attenuation model

                                      |  rates of occurrence

                                      |  CHOSEN exceedance level
   ---------------------------------  |  ---------------------------------
   CHANGING THE BASIS CHANGES THE DESIGN OBLIGATION, THE COST AND THE
   POLITICS --- BEFORE IT CHANGES ANY ROCK.
